<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We resolve the smooth anisotropic Calderón uniqueness problem with arbitrary same-patch measurements. A smooth Riemannian metric on a compact connected manifold of dimension at least three is determined, up to a diffeomorphism fixing the measured patch, by its zero-frequency Dirichlet-to-Neumann energy form with both input and observation on any nonempty open boundary patch.

- **claim-patch-uniqueness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-patch)): Let $n\ge3$. Let $M$ be a compact connected smooth $n$-manifold with nonempty smooth boundary, and let $\Gamma\subset\partial M$ be nonempty, proper, and relatively open. If two smooth Riemannian metrics $g_1,g_2$ on $M$ satisfy $$\Lambda_{g_1,\Gamma}=\Lambda_{g_2,\Gamma},$$ then there is a smooth diffeomorphism $\Phi:M\to M$, smooth through the boundary, such that $$\Phi\|_\Gamma=\mathrm{Id},\qquad g_2=\Phi^*g_1.$$
  - Explanation: Two smooth Riemannian metrics on a compact connected manifold of dimension at least three, with equal local energy Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps on a proper open boundary patch, differ by a smooth diffeomorphism that fixes the patch pointwise.
- **claim-full-boundary** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-full)): Let $M,g_1,g_2$ satisfy the geometric hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a>. If their Dirichlet energies agree for every pair of smooth boundary values, there is a smooth diffeomorphism $F:M\to M$ fixing $\partial M$ pointwise and satisfying $g_1=F^*g_2$.
  - Explanation: Agreement of Dirichlet energies for all smooth boundary values gives a diffeomorphism fixing the whole boundary. The proof applies the patch theorem to the boundary with one point removed and uses continuity.
- **claim-scalar-uniqueness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-scalar)): Let $n\ge3$, and let $\Omega$ and $\Gamma$ be as above. If two strictly positive conductivities $\gamma_1,\gamma_2\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$ satisfy $$\Lambda_{\gamma_1}^\Gamma f=\Lambda_{\gamma_2}^\Gamma f \quad\text{for every }f\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma),$$ then $\gamma_1=\gamma_2$ on $\overline\Omega$.
  - Explanation: Smooth strictly positive scalar conductivities on a Euclidean domain are uniquely determined by the local conductivity map on one nonempty open boundary patch, because conformal self-maps fixing a patch are trivial.
- **claim-boundary-jets** (theorem, [source](#stmt-data-jets)): Let $g_1,g_2$ be smooth metrics on a compact connected smooth $n$-manifold $M$ with nonempty boundary, where $n\ge3$. Suppose their energy maps agree for inputs and observations in a nonempty relatively open set $\Gamma\subset\partial M$. In boundary-normal coordinates for the respective metrics, based on the same coordinates on $\Gamma$, their complete boundary Taylor jets agree.
  - Explanation: Equality of the energy maps on an open patch forces the complete boundary Taylor jets of the two metrics to agree there, via a symbol recursion for the boundary factorization.
- **claim-common-cap** (theorem, [source](#stmt-data-cap)): Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#data:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jets">4</a>, there are compact connected smooth extensions $(M_j^e,g_j^e)$ of $(M,g_j)$ and an identified connected open exterior cap $E$ on which the extended metrics agree. The cap is attached through a nonempty open set $P_0\Subset\Gamma$. The extended zero-Dirichlet Green kernels satisfy $$G_1(x,y)=G_2(x,y),\qquad x,y\in E,\quad x\ne y.$$ For every $F\in C_c^\infty(E)$, the two extended zero-Dirichlet solutions with source $F$ agree on $E$.
  - Explanation: Matching jets let one attach a common exterior cap to both manifolds. The extended Dirichlet Green kernels then agree on the cap away from the diagonal.
- **claim-separation-jets** (theorem, [source](#stmt-data-jet-family)): Let $(N,g)$ be a compact connected smooth manifold with nonempty boundary and dimension $n\ge2$. Let $U\Subset N^\circ$ be nonempty and open, and let $R=L_{g,D}^{-1}$. Then:* 1. *The functionals $I(p):f\mapsto Rf(p)$, $f\in C_c^\infty(U)$, separate distinct interior points. They are nonzero in the interior and zero on the boundary. For each fixed $f$, they depend continuously on $p\in N$.* 2. *If $p\in N^\circ\setminus\overline U$, the second jets of $Rf$ at $p$ fill exactly the hyperplane defined by $L_g u(p)=0$. In particular they provide harmonic coordinates near $p$ and any finite spanning family of harmonic Hessians in those coordinates.
  - Explanation: Source evaluations from the cap separate interior points, and away from the source set their second jets fill exactly the hyperplane allowed by the harmonic equation.
- **claim-local-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-extension)): Let $n\geq3$. Let $(N_1,g_1)$ and $(N_2,g_2)$ be smooth compact connected Riemannian manifolds with nonempty smooth boundary, with Dirichlet Green functions $G_1,G_2$. Identify two interior harmonic coordinate neighborhoods with one neighborhood $\Omega\subset \mathbb R^n$ of $0$. Suppose an open $W\subset\Omega$ contains, near $0$, one side of a smooth hypersurface through $0$, and suppose $$g_1=g_2\text{ on }W,\qquad G_1(x,y)=G_2(x,y)\quad(x,y\in W,\ x\ne y).$$ Suppose there are finitely many smooth pairs $u_j^a$ harmonic for $g_j$ on $\Omega$, agreeing on $W$, whose first-side coordinate Hessians at $0$ span $$\{H\in\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb R^n):g_1^{ij}(0)H_{ij}=0\}.$$ Then the two coordinate metrics agree on a neighborhood of $0$.
  - Explanation: If two metrics, their Green functions and a spanning finite family of paired harmonic functions agree on one side of a smooth hypersurface, the metrics agree on a neighborhood of the contact point.
- **claim-cross-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cross-series)): Let $D_i$ be bounded connected coordinate domains and $S_i\Subset D_i$ nonempty open sets. Suppose a product solution $F$ is given consistently on $$(D_1\times S_2)\cup(S_1\times D_2),$$ with the sum of its two $L^2$ norms at most $M$. Let $O_i\Subset D_i$ be open sets such that every $L_i$-solution $w$ on $D_i$ satisfies $$\label{cross:interpolation-input} \\|w\\|_{L^2(O_i)} \le C\\|w\\|_{L^2(S_i)}^{\gamma_i} \\|w\\|_{L^2(D_i)}^{1-\gamma_i}, \qquad 0<\gamma_i<1,\qquad \gamma_1+\gamma_2>1.$$ Then $F$ extends as a product solution to $O_1\times O_2$, agreeing with both given pieces on their intersections. On every compact subset of this product its $C^m$ norm is at most $C_mM$. The constants are uniform when the geometric margins, ellipticity, coefficient bounds, interpolation constants and positive exponent margin are uniform. Each specified output order uses only finitely many coefficient bounds.
  - Explanation: A product solution given on a cross extends to a product of smaller sets when the single-variable interpolation exponents sum to more than one, with bounds controlled by the data.
- **claim-product-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cross-criterion)): Let $\rho$ be smooth near $z_0=(x_0,y_0)$, with $\rho(z_0)=0$ and $\mathrm d_x\rho,\mathrm d_y\rho\ne0$. Put $$a_i=\frac{\nabla_i\rho}{\|\nabla_i\rho\|^2},\qquad H=\operatorname{Hess}\rho.$$ Suppose that at $z_0$ $$\label{cross:hessian-test} H((a_1,-a_2),(a_1,-a_2)) +H((v_1,v_2),(v_1,v_2))>0 \quad \left(v_i\perp a_i,\ \|v_i\|=\|a_i\|\right).$$ Every product solution given on $\{\rho>0\}$ near $z_0$ extends to a neighborhood of $z_0$, agreeing on a smaller part of that side. In fact, the construction uses only two compact product sets in $\{\rho>\epsilon\}$ for some $\epsilon>0$. The neighborhoods and estimates on smaller neighborhoods are uniform under small smooth perturbations preserving the strict hypotheses, provided the data on those compact sets are bounded.
  - Explanation: A strict Hessian positivity condition on the product defining function lets a product solution given on one side extend across the point, with estimates stable under small perturbations.
- **claim-cylinder-propagation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cross-propagation)): Suppose a region in a product patch has smooth coordinates $(z,a)$ near $B\times[a_-,a_+]$, where $B$ is a compact base, possibly with boundary or corners. A product solution is given on a neighborhood of the top and lateral boundary and on an open set $\mathcal S$. Assume that $\mathcal S$ is upward closed in $a$ at fixed $z$ and contains these boundary neighborhoods. If every level $a=\text{constant}$ satisfies Proposition <a href="#cross:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cross:criterion">10</a> outside $\mathcal S$, with the positive side pointing toward increasing $a$, the solution extends to a neighborhood of the cylinder, agreeing on $\mathcal S$. For fixed coordinates, compact sets and strict reference inequalities, the extension has uniform compact-subset bounds under small smooth perturbations, in terms of bounds on finitely many compact subsets of the initially given region.
  - Explanation: If every level of a compact cylinder satisfies the product criterion outside the seed set, the solution extends through the whole cylinder with uniform bounds.
- **claim-fixed-separation-init** (construction, [source](#stmt-cross-initial)): In this setting, let $n\ge3$ and fix $B>0$, $d>0$ and $\eta>0$. For all sufficiently small $\lambda$, the data <a href="#cross:mixed-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cross:mixed-data">[cross:mixed-data]</a> have a product-solution continuation to a neighborhood of $$\{(x,y):\|x\|,\|y\|\le B,\ \|x-y\|\ge d\}.$$ Every fixed $C^m$ norm on a smaller neighborhood is bounded uniformly in $\lambda$. The continuation agrees with $G_{1,\lambda}$ where $y_n<-\eta$ and with $G_{2,\lambda}$ where $x_n<-\eta$, on the corresponding overlaps. The smallness threshold for $\lambda$ and the constants may depend on $B,d,\eta,m$.
  - Explanation: The mixed Green kernel continues, with bounds uniform in the small dilation, to every compact region at fixed positive separation from the diagonal.
- **claim-profile-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-balls-profiles)): On $Y$, after the preceding choices of geometry, the following bounds hold uniformly for small $\lambda$, $0<\delta\leq1$, and $p\geq2$: $$\label{balls:profile-estimates} \beta_\delta\geq c p,\qquad \operatorname{Hess}_\gamma f_\delta\leq-c\beta_\delta\gamma, \qquad \|\partial^j f_\delta\|\leq C_j\beta_\delta^j \quad(j\geq1).$$ The constants $c,C_j$ do not depend on $p$. The functions $r_\delta^{1/p}$ have a Lipschitz bound independent of $\delta$ and $\lambda$ for fixed $p,R_*$. The radii increase with $\delta$, tend to zero on $\{t\leq0\}$ as $\delta\downarrow0$, and satisfy $$\label{balls:radius-smallness} \sup_{\delta,y}r_\delta\beta_\delta^2\longrightarrow0 \quad\text{as }R_*\downarrow0$$ for each fixed $p$.
  - Explanation: The logarithmic radius profile has gradient bounded below, strictly negative Hessian and controlled higher derivatives, with constants independent of the exponent.
- **claim-sphere-continuation** (construction, [source](#stmt-balls-continuation)): Choose $p$ sufficiently large and then $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small in terms of the fixed background bounds. Choose $R_*>0$ small enough that all the balls lie in normal neighborhoods and <a href="#balls:radius-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:radius-smallness">[balls:radius-smallness]</a> is at most $\varepsilon$. For all sufficiently small spatial dilations $\lambda$, and every $0<\delta\le1$ for which <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a> holds, the mixed kernel extends to a neighborhood of $$\{(x,y):y\in Y,\ d_\gamma(x,y)=r_\delta(y)\}.$$ The extension solves the two homogeneous equations and agrees with the prescribed lower cross and the fixed-separation continuation. It is smooth for each positive $\delta$; no uniform norm as $\delta\downarrow0$ is asserted here.
  - Explanation: Under the temporary smallness condition the mixed kernel continues to the family of shrinking spheres; smoothness is asserted for each positive parameter only.
- **claim-transfer-density** (construction, [source](#stmt-balls-functional)): Under the assumptions of Proposition <a href="#balls:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:continuation">14</a>, there is a smooth function $\psi(y,\omega)$ on the unit sphere bundle over $Y^\circ$ such that $$\label{balls:density-representation} T_yu=\int_{S_y}\psi(y,\omega) u\bigl(\exp_y^\gamma(r(y)\omega)\bigr)\,d\sigma_y(\omega).$$ For every fixed center $y_0$, if $u$ is first-metric harmonic on a neighborhood of $\overline{B_\gamma(y_0,r(y_0))}$, then $y\mapsto T_yu$ is defined and second-metric harmonic on a sufficiently small neighborhood of $y_0$. This neighborhood may depend on $u$. In particular, for functions harmonic on the fixed coordinate neighborhood required by the sphere family, $$\label{balls:reproduction} \int_{S_y}\psi\,d\sigma_y=1, \qquad T_yx^i=y^i, \qquad T_yu_{1,\lambda}^a=u_{2,\lambda}^a(y).$$ On the support of derivatives of $\chi$, every fixed angular smooth norm of $\psi$ and its first base derivatives is bounded independently of $\delta$ and small $\lambda$, after $p,R_*$ and the fixed geometry have been chosen.
  - Explanation: The flux functional is represented by a smooth density on the unit sphere bundle and reproduces constants, coordinates and the paired harmonic functions.
- **claim-moving-trace** (theorem, [source](#stmt-angular-trace)): Suppose that the normal balls and the functional $T_y$ of Proposition <a href="#balls:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:functional">15</a> are defined, and that $$\label{angular:smallness} \sum_{\|\alpha\|\le8}\|\partial_y^\alpha h\|\le\varepsilon r, \qquad r\beta^2\le\varepsilon.$$ If $u$ is first-metric harmonic near one such ball, set $$U(y,\omega)=u\bigl(\exp_y^\gamma(r(y)\omega)\bigr).$$ There are sphere operators $\mathcal C_i$ such that $\nabla_iU=\mathcal C_iU$. Their sphere adjoints satisfy $$\label{angular:C-expansion} \mathcal C_i^*=r^{-1}A_i^*+f_iB+E_i+F_i, \qquad E_i\in r\Psi^1,\quad F_i\in\Psi^0, \qquad f_i=\nabla_i f.$$ The normalized remainders $r^{-1}E_i$ and $F_i$ have uniform symbol bounds after up to six base derivatives, with the allowance $C_j\beta^j$.* *Write the normalized second equation, in the reference connection, as $Q^{ij}\nabla_i\nabla_j+b_2^i\nabla_i$. Its drift $b_2$ is uniformly controlled. If $\psi(y,\omega)$ represents $T_y$ against $d\sigma_y$, then $$\label{angular:P-equation} P\psi=0,\qquad P=r\left[Q^{ij}(\nabla_i+\mathcal C_i^*) (\nabla_j+\mathcal C_j^*) +b_2^i(\nabla_i+\mathcal C_i^*)\right].$$ The products in this formula are covariant products. In particular, $Q$ is not differentiated by taking a base adjoint.
  - Explanation: Differentiating the trace of a harmonic function over the moving sphere gives operators with an explicit expansion and an equation satisfied by the transfer density.
- **claim-raising-estimate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-angular-raising)): Let $n\ge3$, and suppose <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a> holds on a fixed base region with uniformly bounded reference geometry. There are $\beta_0$ and $c>0$, depending only on that geometry and the constants in <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a>, such that every smooth, compactly base-supported function with zero fiber mean satisfies $$\label{angular:raising-estimate} \\|T\varphi\\|^2-\\|T^\flat\varphi\\|^2 \ge c\,\mathcal N(\varphi)^2.$$
  - Explanation: A weighted comparison of degree-raising and degree-lowering operators gives a positive estimate, the negative spatial Hessian absorbing bounded curvature errors.
- **claim-coercivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-angular-coercivity)): Fix a base region with bounded reference geometry and the constants in <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a>. Suppose $P$ is the operator <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a>, with the trace operators of Lemma <a href="#angular:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:trace">16</a>. There exist $\beta_0$ sufficiently large, $\varepsilon_0>0$ and $C<\infty$, depending only on these fixed bounds, such that <a href="#angular:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:smallness">[angular:smallness]</a> with $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$ implies $$\label{angular:coercive-estimate} \mathcal N(\varphi)\le C\\|P\varphi\\|$$ for every smooth, compactly base-supported function $\varphi$ with zero mean on each sphere. These constants are independent of the shrinking parameter. Only the eight indicated derivatives of the small tensor $h$ are required.
  - Explanation: The moving trace operator is coercive on mean-zero functions, uniformly as the spheres shrink, once the small tensor is controlled in finitely many derivatives.
- **claim-uniform-density** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-density)): Fix the structural parameters so that Propositions <a href="#balls:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:continuation">14</a> and <a href="#angular:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:coercivity">18</a> apply. There is a constant $C$ independent of $\delta$ and of sufficiently small spatial dilations $\lambda$ such that $$\label{local:density-bound} \\|\chi\psi\\|_{L^2(S_\gamma Y)}\leq C$$ whenever the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a> holds.
  - Explanation: The cutoff transfer density has a norm bound independent of the shrinking parameter and the small dilation.
- **claim-moment-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-moment)): Under the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a>, the paired differences satisfy $$\label{local:moment-bound} \left\\|\frac{\chi V^a}{r_\delta^2}\right\\|_{L^2(Y)}\leq C$$ with a constant independent of $\delta$ and sufficiently small $\lambda$.
  - Explanation: Exact reproduction of affine functions turns the density bound into a weighted norm bound for the paired harmonic differences.
- **claim-finite-interpolation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-interpolation)): Let $V$ be smooth on a neighborhood of $\overline{B(y,s)}\subset \mathbb R^n$, with $0<s\leq1$ and $\\|V\\|_{C^m}\leq A_m$ there. For every multi-index $\alpha$ of length $j<m$, $$\label{local:interpolation-estimate} \|\partial^\alpha V(y)\|\leq C_{m,n} \bigl(s^{-j-n/2}\\|V\\|_{L^2(B(y,s))} +A_m s^{m-j}\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: Pointwise derivatives of a smooth function on a small ball are bounded by its weighted norm on the ball plus a higher-order remainder.
- **claim-superlinear-improvement** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-improvement)): After the choices in <a href="#local:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:exponents">[local:exponents]</a>, there are $C>0$ and $\rho_0>0$, independent of $\delta$ and sufficiently small $\lambda$, such that the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a> implies $$\label{local:superlinear} H_8(y)\leq C r_\delta(y)^{3/2} \quad\text{for }y\in K\text{ with }r_\delta(y)<\rho_0.$$
  - Explanation: The temporary linear bound on the metric discrepancy improves to a strictly superlinear power of the radius at small radii.
- **claim-no-interior-frontier** (theorem, [source](#stmt-global-frontier)): There is an isometry $$\Phi:(M_2^e)^\circ\longrightarrow(M_1^e)^\circ$$ onto the first interior satisfying Equation <a href="#global:matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:matching">[global:matching]</a>.
  - Explanation: The maximal admissible match is an isometry of the whole second interior onto the first interior.
- **claim-smooth-completion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-global-completion)): The isometry of Proposition <a href="#global:frontier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frontier">23</a> extends to a smooth diffeomorphism $M_2^e\to M_1^e$. It preserves the original copies of $M$, and its restriction satisfies $$g_2=\Phi^*g_1,\qquad \Phi\|_\Gamma=\mathrm{Id}.$$
  - Explanation: The interior isometry extends smoothly through the boundary and is the identity on the measured patch.
- **claim-conformal-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity)): Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a connected open set with smooth boundary, where $n\ge3$. Suppose $F:\overline\Omega\to\overline\Omega$ is a smooth diffeomorphism through the boundary and $$F^*e=e^{2b}e\quad\text{in }\Omega$$ for a real $b\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$, where $e$ is the Euclidean metric. If $F$ fixes a nonempty relatively open subset of $\partial\Omega$ pointwise, then $F=\mathrm{Id}$ and $b=0$.
  - Explanation: A conformal self-diffeomorphism of a connected Euclidean domain fixing an open boundary patch pointwise is the identity.

Then:*\n\n1.  *The functionals $I(p):f\\mapsto Rf(p)$, $f\\in C_c^\\infty(U)$, separate distinct interior points. They are nonzero in the interior and zero on the boundary. For each fixed $f$, they depend continuously on $p\\in N$.*\n\n2.  *If $p\\in N^\\circ\\setminus\\overline U$, the second jets of $Rf$ at $p$ fill exactly the hyperplane defined by $L_g u(p)=0$. In particular they provide harmonic coordinates near $p$ and any finite spanning family of harmonic Hessians in those coordinates.",
      "explanation": "Source evaluations from the cap separate interior points, and away from the source set their second jets fill exactly the hyperplane allowed by the harmonic equation.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-local-extension",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n\\geq3$. Let $(N_1,g_1)$ and $(N_2,g_2)$ be smooth compact connected Riemannian manifolds with nonempty smooth boundary, with Dirichlet Green functions $G_1,G_2$. Identify two interior harmonic coordinate neighborhoods with one neighborhood $\\Omega\\subset\n\\mathbb R^n$ of $0$. Suppose an open $W\\subset\\Omega$ contains, near $0$, one side of a smooth hypersurface through $0$, and suppose $$g_1=g_2\\text{ on }W,\\qquad\n G_1(x,y)=G_2(x,y)\\quad(x,y\\in W,\\ x\\ne y).$$ Suppose there are finitely many smooth pairs $u_j^a$ harmonic for $g_j$ on $\\Omega$, agreeing on $W$, whose first-side coordinate Hessians at $0$ span $$\\{H\\in\\operatorname{Sym}^2(\\mathbb R^n):g_1^{ij}(0)H_{ij}=0\\}.$$ Then the two coordinate metrics agree on a neighborhood of $0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-365.2"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cross-series",
        "exact_quote": "Let $D_i$ be bounded connected coordinate domains and $S_i\\Subset D_i$ nonempty open sets. Suppose a product solution $F$ is given consistently on $$(D_1\\times S_2)\\cup(S_1\\times D_2),$$ with the sum of its two $L^2$ norms at most $M$. Let $O_i\\Subset D_i$ be open sets such that every $L_i$-solution $w$ on $D_i$ satisfies $$\\label{cross:interpolation-input}\n \\|w\\|_{L^2(O_i)}\n \\le C\\|w\\|_{L^2(S_i)}^{\\gamma_i}\n          \\|w\\|_{L^2(D_i)}^{1-\\gamma_i},\n \\qquad 0<\\gamma_i<1,\\qquad \\gamma_1+\\gamma_2>1.$$ Then $F$ extends as a product solution to $O_1\\times O_2$, agreeing with both given pieces on their intersections. On every compact subset of this product its $C^m$ norm is at most $C_mM$. The constants are uniform when the geometric margins, ellipticity, coefficient bounds, interpolation constants and positive exponent margin are uniform. Each specified output order uses only finitely many coefficient bounds.",

      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cross-criterion",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\rho$ be smooth near $z_0=(x_0,y_0)$, with $\\rho(z_0)=0$ and $\\mathrm d_x\\rho,\\mathrm d_y\\rho\\ne0$. Put $$a_i=\\frac{\\nabla_i\\rho}{|\\nabla_i\\rho|^2},\\qquad H=\\operatorname{Hess}\\rho.$$ Suppose that at $z_0$ $$\\label{cross:hessian-test}\n H((a_1,-a_2),(a_1,-a_2))\n +H((v_1,v_2),(v_1,v_2))>0\n \\quad\n \\left(v_i\\perp a_i,\\ |v_i|=|a_i|\\right).$$ Every product solution given on $\\{\\rho>0\\}$ near $z_0$ extends to a neighborhood of $z_0$, agreeing on a smaller part of that side. In fact, the construction uses only two compact product sets in $\\{\\rho>\\epsilon\\}$ for some $\\epsilon>0$. The neighborhoods and estimates on smaller neighborhoods are uniform under small smooth perturbations preserving the strict hypotheses, provided the data on those compact sets are bounded.",
        "source_id": "PM-365.2"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cross-propagation",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose a region in a product patch has smooth coordinates $(z,a)$ near $B\\times[a_-,a_+]$, where $B$ is a compact base, possibly with boundary or corners. A product solution is given on a neighborhood of the top and lateral boundary and on an open set $\\mathcal S$. Assume that $\\mathcal S$ is upward closed in $a$ at fixed $z$ and contains these boundary neighborhoods. If every level $a=\\text{constant}$ satisfies Proposition <a href=\"#cross:criterion\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cross:criterion\">10</a> outside $\\mathcal S$, with the positive side pointing toward increasing $a$, the solution extends to a neighborhood of the cylinder, agreeing on $\\mathcal S$. For fixed coordinates, compact sets and strict reference inequalities, the extension has uniform compact-subset bounds under small smooth perturbations, in terms of bounds on finitely many compact subsets of the initially given region.",

        "anchor": "stmt-cross-initial",
        "exact_quote": "In this setting, let $n\\ge3$ and fix $B>0$, $d>0$ and $\\eta>0$. For all sufficiently small $\\lambda$, the data <a href=\"#cross:mixed-data\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cross:mixed-data\">[cross:mixed-data]</a> have a product-solution continuation to a neighborhood of $$\\{(x,y):|x|,|y|\\le B,\\ |x-y|\\ge d\\}.$$ Every fixed $C^m$ norm on a smaller neighborhood is bounded uniformly in $\\lambda$. The continuation agrees with $G_{1,\\lambda}$ where $y_n<-\\eta$ and with $G_{2,\\lambda}$ where $x_n<-\\eta$, on the corresponding overlaps.         "anchor": "stmt-balls-profiles",
        "exact_quote": "On $Y$, after the preceding choices of geometry, the following bounds hold uniformly for small $\\lambda$, $0<\\delta\\leq1$, and $p\\geq2$: $$\\label{balls:profile-estimates}\n \\beta_\\delta\\geq c p,\\qquad\n \\operatorname{Hess}_\\gamma f_\\delta\\leq-c\\beta_\\delta\\gamma,\n \\qquad |\\partial^j f_\\delta|\\leq C_j\\beta_\\delta^j\n \\quad(j\\geq1).$$ The constants $c,C_j$ do not depend on $p$. The functions $r_\\delta^{1/p}$ have a Lipschitz bound independent of $\\delta$ and $\\lambda$ for fixed $p,R_*$. The radii increase with $\\delta$, tend to zero on $\\{t\\leq0\\}$ as $\\delta\\downarrow0$, and satisfy $$\\label{balls:radius-smallness}\n \\sup_{\\delta,y}r_\\delta\\beta_\\delta^2\\longrightarrow0\n \\quad\\text{as }R_*\\downarrow0$$ for each fixed $p$.",

It is smooth for each positive $\\delta$; no uniform norm as $\\delta\\downarrow0$ is asserted here.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-balls-functional",
        "exact_quote": "Under the assumptions of Proposition <a href=\"#balls:continuation\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"balls:continuation\">14</a>, there is a smooth function $\\psi(y,\\omega)$ on the unit sphere bundle over $Y^\\circ$ such that $$\\label{balls:density-representation}\n T_yu=\\int_{S_y}\\psi(y,\\omega)\n              u\\bigl(\\exp_y^\\gamma(r(y)\\omega)\\bigr)\\,d\\sigma_y(\\omega).$$ For every fixed center $y_0$, if $u$ is first-metric harmonic on a neighborhood of $\\overline{B_\\gamma(y_0,r(y_0))}$, then $y\\mapsto T_yu$ is defined and second-metric harmonic on a sufficiently small neighborhood of $y_0$. This neighborhood may depend on $u$. In particular, for functions harmonic on the fixed coordinate neighborhood required by the sphere family, $$\\label{balls:reproduction}\n \\int_{S_y}\\psi\\,d\\sigma_y=1,\n \\qquad T_yx^i=y^i,\n \\qquad T_yu_{1,\\lambda}^a=u_{2,\\lambda}^a(y).$$ On the support of derivatives of $\\chi$, every fixed angular smooth norm of $\\psi$ and its first base derivatives is bounded independently of $\\delta$ and small $\\lambda$, after $p,R_*$ and the fixed geometry have been chosen.",

      },

        "exact_quote": "Suppose that the normal balls and the functional $T_y$ of Proposition <a href=\"#balls:functional\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"balls:functional\">15</a> are defined, and that $$\\label{angular:smallness}\n \\sum_{|\\alpha|\\le8}|\\partial_y^\\alpha h|\\le\\varepsilon r,\n \\qquad r\\beta^2\\le\\varepsilon.$$ If $u$ is first-metric harmonic near one such ball, set $$U(y,\\omega)=u\\bigl(\\exp_y^\\gamma(r(y)\\omega)\\bigr).$$ There are sphere operators $\\mathcal C_i$ such that $\\nabla_iU=\\mathcal C_iU$. Their sphere adjoints satisfy $$\\label{angular:C-expansion}\n \\mathcal C_i^*=r^{-1}A_i^*+f_iB+E_i+F_i,\n \\qquad E_i\\in r\\Psi^1,\\quad F_i\\in\\Psi^0,\n \\qquad f_i=\\nabla_i f.$$ The normalized remainders $r^{-1}E_i$ and $F_i$ have uniform symbol bounds after up to six base derivatives, with the allowance $C_j\\beta^j$.*\n\n\n*Write the normalized second equation, in the reference connection, as $Q^{ij}\\nabla_i\\nabla_j+b_2^i\\nabla_i$. Its drift $b_2$ is uniformly controlled. If $\\psi(y,\\omega)$ represents $T_y$ against $d\\sigma_y$, then $$\\label{angular:P-equation}\n P\\psi=0,\\qquad\n P=r\\left[Q^{ij}(\\nabla_i+\\mathcal C_i^*)\n                    (\\nabla_j+\\mathcal C_j^*)\n             +b_2^i(\\nabla_i+\\mathcal C_i^*)\\right].$$ The products in this formula are covariant products. In particular, $Q$ is not differentiated by taking a base adjoint.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Laplace-Beltrami convention** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Here $\Delta_g=\operatorname{div}_g\nabla_g$.
- **Paired harmonic difference** ([source](#pm-h-33)): Write $V^a=v_{2,\lambda}^a-v_{1,\lambda}^a$.

| q-superlinear-bound | metric discrepancy H_8 | ≤ | $C r_\delta(y)^{3/2}$ | Independent of the shrinking parameter, small dilation |
| q-rho-choice | threshold radius condition | ≤ | $\varepsilon/2$ | Radius below the improvement threshold |
| q-density-mass | total mass of the transfer density | = | $1$ | Every center in the cylinder |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-data-jets** [claim](#stmt-data-jets) / [proof](#proof-stmt-data-jets): Symbol calculus for the boundary factorization and induction on normal jets, with polarization of the highest-jet term. -> equal complete boundary Taylor jets
- **step-data-cap** [claim](#stmt-data-cap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-data-cap): Paste a common exterior cap along a bump graph and compare Dirichlet minimizers by an energy replacement argument. -> common cap and equal Green kernels there
- **step-jet-family** [claim](#stmt-data-jet-family) / [proof](#proof-stmt-data-jet-family): Duality with point-supported distributions, unique continuation and ellipticity of the order count. -> point separation and spanning harmonic jets
- **step-cross-series** [claim](#stmt-cross-series) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cross-series): Singular value expansion of the restriction map and convergence of the product series from interpolation exponents. -> extension to a product of smaller sets
- **step-cross-criterion** [claim](#stmt-cross-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cross-criterion): Convex separation splitting the Hessian condition into factor weights, Carleman estimates and the cross lemma. -> product extension across a point
- **step-cross-propagation** [claim](#stmt-cross-propagation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cross-propagation): Descending induction on the level parameter, gluing local germs by unique continuation on overlaps. -> extension through the compact cylinder
- **step-cross-initial** [claim](#stmt-cross-initial) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cross-initial): Cross the limiting plane by two-constants estimates, then sweep variable-radius tubes inward with the propagation lemma. -> continuation at fixed separation from the diagonal
- **step-profile-bounds** [claim](#stmt-balls-profiles) / [proof](#proof-stmt-balls-profiles): Explicit calculus for the smoothed logarithmic profile and the conformal Hessian formula. -> gradient, Hessian and derivative bounds
- **step-sphere-continuation** [claim](#stmt-balls-continuation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-balls-continuation): Verify the strict extension test at every moving sphere, then propagate over the parameter interval. -> mixed kernel on the sphere family
- **step-transfer-density** [claim](#stmt-balls-functional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-balls-functional): Adjunction of the Dirichlet solution operator, Green identity on a fixed surface and unique continuation for reproduction. -> smooth transfer density with exact affine reproduction
- **step-moving-trace** [claim](#stmt-angular-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-angular-trace): Parametrix expansion of the radial derivative and differentiation of the pairing to obtain the density equation. -> expansion of trace operators and equation for the density
- **step-raising-estimate** [claim](#stmt-angular-raising) / [proof](#proof-stmt-angular-raising): Fischer inner product on harmonic polynomials, integrations by parts and a convex combination of bounds. -> weighted raising estimate
- **step-coercivity** [claim](#stmt-angular-coercivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-angular-coercivity): Norm comparison keeping principal perturbations, commutator counts showing no third base derivative survives. -> coercive estimate for the trace operator
- **step-uniform-density** [claim](#stmt-local-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-density): Apply coercivity to the mean-zero part of the density using cutoff commutator bounds. -> uniform norm bound for the density
- **step-moment-bound** [claim](#stmt-local-moment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-moment): Cancel the affine Taylor polynomial using exact reproduction and bound the remainder on the sphere. -> two-power bound for paired differences
- **step-finite-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-local-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-interpolation): Taylor expansion on the unit ball and equivalence of norms on polynomials. -> pointwise derivative bound
- **step-superlinear-improvement** [claim](#stmt-local-improvement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-improvement): Combine the moment bound, interpolation and inversion of the harmonic Hessian system on a small ball. -> strictly superlinear bound for the metric discrepancy
- **step-local-extension** [claim](#stmt-local-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-improvement-2): Continuity method in the shrinking parameter with one fixed dilation, then conformal rigidity from common harmonic coordinates. -> metrics agree near the contact point
- **step-no-interior-frontier** [claim](#stmt-global-frontier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-frontier): Maximal admissible match, separation of evaluations, touching ball at a frontier point and the local extension theorem. -> isometry between full interiors
- **step-smooth-completion** [claim](#stmt-global-completion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-completion): Metric completion, smoothness through boundary normal flow and boundary unique continuation for equal densities. -> diffeomorphism fixing the patch
- **step-conformal-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity): Boundary initial values from the connection formula, then flatness gives an ordinary differential equation with zero data. -> identity map and vanishing conformal factor
- **step-main-patch** [claim](#stmt-main-patch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-completion-2): Chain the boundary jets, common cap, harmonic tests, local extension and completion. -> patch-fixing diffeomorphism pulling back the metric
- **step-full-boundary** [claim](#stmt-main-full) / [proof](#proof-stmt-main-full): Apply the patch theorem to the boundary minus a point and use continuity. -> diffeomorphism fixing the whole boundary
- **step-scalar-uniqueness** [claim](#stmt-main-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity-2): Convert conductivities to conformal metrics, apply the patch theorem and then conformal rigidity. -> equal conductivities

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-patch-gauge-uniqueness**: For smooth metrics on a compact connected manifold of dimension at least three, equality of the local energy Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps on a nonempty proper relatively open boundary patch forces the metrics to differ only by a smooth diffeomorphism that fixes the patch pointwise.
- **inv-local-extension-dimension**: The local metric extension across a hypersurface is proved only for dimension at least three, using agreement of metrics, Green functions and a spanning family of harmonic functions on one side.
- **inv-conformal-rigidity-patch**: A conformal self-diffeomorphism of a connected Euclidean domain of dimension at least three that fixes a boundary patch pointwise is the identity.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-planar-case**: Applying the uniqueness theorem to planar anisotropic tomography. -> The result is stated and proved only for dimension at least three; nothing is claimed in dimension two.
- **anti-rough-conductivity**: Applying the scalar corollary to rough or merely bounded conductivities. -> The corollary needs conductivities smooth up to the boundary and strictly positive; the argument uses jets and smooth continuation.
- **anti-disjoint-patches**: Using the theorem when excitation and measurement occur on different boundary patches. -> The measured map pairs inputs and observations on one common patch; disjoint patches give a different datum not covered.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The paper proves uniqueness theorems by analytic continuation, Carleman estimates, spectral expansions and compactness arguments; no finite procedure with explicit steps or computable output is stated, so there is nothing for a runnable reference and oracle to decide.

The steps that come closest to a procedure are a continuity argument in the parameter $\delta$ and a maximal-extension argument. Neither is an effective finite construction, so no pseudocode or complexity is justified. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-dimension-three** (hypothesis): The main theorem and the local extension theorem are proved only for dimension at least three. The final conformal step uses that the factor n-2 is nonzero, and no claim is made in dimension two. Blocked use: ['Applying the uniqueness theorem to planar anisotropic tomography', 'Citing the result for two-dimensional conformal gauge problems']
- **limit-smoothness-up-to-boundary** (hypothesis): Metrics are smooth up to the boundary. Boundary jet recovery, interior continuation and finite-order interpolation all use smooth coefficients, so rough coefficients are outside the stated result. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to bounded measurable or piecewise-constant conductivities', 'Using jet recovery for metrics of limited regularity']
- **limit-same-patch-data** (hypothesis): Input and observation are confined to one common nonempty relatively open proper boundary patch. The cap attachment and Green kernel comparison rely on this shared patch. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem for excitation and measurement on disjoint boundary patches', 'Treating the one-patch datum as covering transmission geometries']
- **limit-diffeomorphism-gauge** (formal_scope): For general metrics the conclusion is determination only up to a smooth diffeomorphism fixing the patch; the metric itself is not recovered in fixed coordinates. Blocked use: ['Claiming recovery of an anisotropic metric in fixed coordinates', 'Reading the result as determination of an anisotropic tensor without gauge']
- **limit-exact-data** (approximation): The hypothesis is exact equality of the maps. No stability estimate or noise-robust statement is given, and the constants in the estimates are not made explicit. Blocked use: ['Citing the theorem as a guarantee for noisy or discretised data', 'Deriving quantitative reconstruction error bounds from it']
- **limit-shrinking-sphere-no-uniform** (edge_case): The sphere-family continuation is smooth for each positive parameter, with no uniform norm asserted as the parameter tends to zero. Uniform control needs the separate coercive estimate under the temporary smallness condition. Blocked use: ['Assuming uniform bounds on the continued kernel as the radius shrinks', 'Dropping the temporary coefficient condition in the density estimate']
- **limit-nonconstructive-proof** (nonconstructive): The proof is an existence argument: a continuity argument in the shrinking parameter and a maximal admissible match. No finite procedure with computable output is given. Blocked use: ['Reading the maximal-match argument as an algorithm with a stopping rule', 'Implementing the proof as a reconstruction routine']
- **limit-rough-scalar-contrast** (counterexample_scope): In dimension three, bounded measurable conductivities equal to one near the boundary can share the same full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, so smoothness cannot be dropped. Blocked use: ['Extending the scalar corollary to rough conductivities']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the smooth anisotropic Calderón uniqueness problem with arbitrary same-patch measurements. A smooth Riemannian metric on a compact connected manifold of dimension at least three is determined, up to a diffeomorphism fixing the measured patch, by its zero-frequency Dirichlet-to-Neumann energy form with both input and observation on any nonempty open boundary patch.

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

Calderón’s inverse boundary problem asks whether boundary measurements determine the coefficients of an elliptic equation. In its geometric form, the unknown is a Riemannian metric and the measurements are the Dirichlet energy of harmonic extensions. A change of interior coordinates that fixes the measured boundary points preserves these measurements. We prove that this is the only ambiguity for smooth metrics in dimension at least three, even when both the prescribed values and the observations are confined to one arbitrary open boundary patch.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The measurement and the theorem

Let $M$ be a compact connected smooth $n$-manifold with nonempty smooth boundary, and let $g$ be a smooth positive-definite Riemannian metric on $M$, including its boundary. All volume and surface integrals use densities, so no orientation is required. For $f\in H^{1/2}(\partial M)$, let $u_f^g\in H^1(M)$ be the unique weak solution of $$-\Delta_g u_f^g=0\quad\text{in }M,\qquad
 u_f^g|_{\partial M}=f.$$ Here $\Delta_g=\operatorname{div}_g\nabla_g$. For a nonempty relatively open set $\Gamma\subset\partial M$, put $$H^{1/2}_{co}(\Gamma)
 =\{f\in H^{1/2}(\partial M):\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f\subset\Gamma\}.$$ Equip this space with the norm inherited from $H^{1/2}(\partial M)$. The local energy Dirichlet-to-Neumann map is the bounded operator $$\Lambda_{g,\Gamma}:H^{1/2}_{co}(\Gamma)
       \longrightarrow (H^{1/2}_{co}(\Gamma))^*$$ defined by <a id="intro:energy"></a>$$\label{intro:energy}
 \langle\Lambda_{g,\Gamma}f,h\rangle
 =\int_M\langle\mathrm du_f^g,\mathrm du_h^g\rangle_g\,\mathrm dV_g.$$ For smooth supported data, Green’s formula writes this as <a id="intro:density"></a>$$\label{intro:density}
 \langle\Lambda_{g,\Gamma}f,h\rangle
 =\int_\Gamma h\,\partial_{\nu_g}u_f^g\,\mathrm dS_g,
 \qquad f,h\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma),$$ where $\nu_g$ is the outward unit normal. Thus the measured output includes its boundary density. Equality on smooth supported data is equivalent to equality of the maps: each element of $H^{1/2}_{co}(\Gamma)$ has compact support in $\Gamma$ and can be approximated in $H^{1/2}(\partial M)$ by smooth functions supported in a fixed larger compact subset of $\Gamma$.

<a id="stmt-main-patch"></a>
<div id="main:patch" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Smooth anisotropic uniqueness from one patch). *Let $n\ge3$. Let $M$ be a compact connected smooth $n$-manifold with nonempty smooth boundary, and let $\Gamma\subset\partial M$ be nonempty, proper, and relatively open. If two smooth Riemannian metrics $g_1,g_2$ on $M$ satisfy $$\Lambda_{g_1,\Gamma}=\Lambda_{g_2,\Gamma},$$ then there is a smooth diffeomorphism $\Phi:M\to M$, smooth through the boundary, such that $$\Phi|_\Gamma=\mathrm{Id},\qquad g_2=\Phi^*g_1.$$*

</div>

Equality of the graphs of these operators is equivalent to the hypothesis. Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a> resolves positively the smooth anisotropic Calderón uniqueness problem with arbitrary same-patch measurements. The datum is the density-valued energy map defined above. The boundary may have several components and the manifold may be nonorientable. The conclusion fixes every measured boundary point; it imposes no pointwise condition on the inaccessible boundary.

The diffeomorphism ambiguity is unavoidable. If $\Phi$ fixes $\Gamma$ and $g_2=\Phi^*g_1$, then for $f$ supported in $\Gamma$ the pullback $u_f^{g_1}\circ\Phi$ has boundary value $f$. Indeed a boundary diffeomorphism fixing $\Gamma$ also preserves its complement. Change of variables in the energy proves equality of the local maps.

<a id="stmt-main-full"></a>
<div id="main:full" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Full-boundary uniqueness). *Let $M,g_1,g_2$ satisfy the geometric hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a>. If their Dirichlet energies agree for every pair of smooth boundary values, there is a smooth diffeomorphism $F:M\to M$ fixing $\partial M$ pointwise and satisfying $g_1=F^*g_2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-main-full"><summary>Proof of stmt-main-full</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $p\in\partial M$ and apply Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a> to $\Gamma=\partial M\setminus\{p\}$. This is a nonempty proper open patch, dense in the entire boundary. Continuity makes the resulting $\Phi$ the identity on $\partial M$. Take $F=\Phi^{-1}$. ◻

</details>

There is also a consequence with no coordinate ambiguity. Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be bounded and connected with smooth boundary, let $\Gamma\subset\partial\Omega$ be nonempty and relatively open, and let $\gamma\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$ be strictly positive. For $f\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma)$, extended by zero to the rest of the boundary, solve $$\operatorname{div}(\gamma\nabla u_f^\gamma)=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,
 \qquad u_f^\gamma|_{\partial\Omega}=f.$$ With $\nu_e$ the outward Euclidean unit normal, define the local conductivity map by $$\Lambda_\gamma^\Gamma f
       =\left.\gamma\partial_{\nu_e}u_f^\gamma\right|_\Gamma.$$

<a id="stmt-main-scalar"></a>
<div id="main:scalar" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Smooth scalar uniqueness from one patch). *Let $n\ge3$, and let $\Omega$ and $\Gamma$ be as above. If two strictly positive conductivities $\gamma_1,\gamma_2\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$ satisfy $$\Lambda_{\gamma_1}^\Gamma f=\Lambda_{\gamma_2}^\Gamma f
       \quad\text{for every }f\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma),$$ then $\gamma_1=\gamma_2$ on $\overline\Omega$.*

</div>

The reduction in Section <a href="#scalar:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:section">8</a> identifies the exact metric energy associated with a scalar conductivity. It then proves that a conformal self-diffeomorphism of the domain fixing a boundary patch must be the identity. No conductivity values on the inaccessible boundary are assumed.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Historical context

Calderón formulated the inverse conductivity problem through boundary measurements of Dirichlet energy and studied its linearization (Calderón 1980) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Calderon1980](#ref-Calderon1980)]. Kohn and Vogelius established scalar boundary determination (Kohn and Vogelius 1984) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KohnVogelius1984](#ref-KohnVogelius1984)]. Sylvester and Uhlmann proved full-data uniqueness for smooth scalar conductivities in Euclidean dimensions at least three using complex geometrical optics solutions (Sylvester and Uhlmann 1987) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[SylvesterUhlmann1987](#ref-SylvesterUhlmann1987)]. For anisotropic conductivities, Lee and Uhlmann developed boundary determination and proved real-analytic recovery under geometric and topological hypotheses (Lee and Uhlmann 1989) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LeeUhlmann1989](#ref-LeeUhlmann1989)]. Lassas and Uhlmann subsequently recovered a real-analytic manifold and metric from an arbitrary nonempty real-analytic boundary patch in dimensions at least three, without the earlier topological restrictions (Lassas and Uhlmann 2001, Theorem 1.1(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[LassasUhlmann2001](#ref-LassasUhlmann2001)]. Their Green-function method also led to the complete-manifold extension of Lassas, Taylor, and Uhlmann (Lassas, Taylor, and Uhlmann 2003) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[LassasTaylorUhlmann2003](#ref-LassasTaylorUhlmann2003)]. Guillarmou and Sá Barreto proved same-patch uniqueness for smooth compact Einstein manifolds with the same Einstein constant, recovering a boundary neighborhood from the Einstein equation before applying analytic continuation in the interior (Guillarmou and Sá Barreto 2009, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GuillarmouSaBarreto2009](#ref-GuillarmouSaBarreto2009)].

For smooth scalar partial data, Bukhgeim and Uhlmann introduced a Carleman-based recovery method (Bukhgeim and Uhlmann 2002) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BukhgeimUhlmann2002](#ref-BukhgeimUhlmann2002)]. Kenig, Sjöstrand, and Uhlmann prescribed input near a back face and observation near a front face defined by a point outside the convex hull (Kenig, Sjöstrand, and Uhlmann 2007, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KenigSjostrandUhlmann2007](#ref-KenigSjostrandUhlmann2007)]; their conductivity corollary also assumes equality on the whole boundary (Kenig, Sjöstrand, and Uhlmann 2007, Corollary 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KenigSjostrandUhlmann2007](#ref-KenigSjostrandUhlmann2007)]. The two measurement regions are constrained by this geometry. In dimension three, Isakov obtained same-patch uniqueness by reflection when the inaccessible boundary lies in a plane or sphere (Isakov 2007) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Isakov2007](#ref-Isakov2007)]. Dos Santos Ferreira, Kenig, Sjöstrand, and Uhlmann proved the density-of-products theorem for the linearized Schrödinger same-patch problem at zero potential (Dos Santos Ferreira, Kenig, Sjöstrand, et al. 2009, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[DosSantosFerreiraEtAl2009](#ref-DosSantosFerreiraEtAl2009)]. Alessandrini and Kim obtained same-patch stability for scalar conductivities known near the whole boundary (Alessandrini and Kim 2012) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[AlessandriniKim2012](#ref-AlessandriniKim2012)].

Smooth geometric results also exploit special backgrounds. Dos Santos Ferreira, Kenig, Salo, and Uhlmann developed limiting Carleman weights and recovered potentials and conformal factors on admissible manifolds, which are conformal to submanifolds of a product with a simple transverse factor (Dos Santos Ferreira, Kenig, Salo, et al. 2009) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DSFKSU2009](#ref-DSFKSU2009)]. Kenig and Salo extended partial-data methods on such product geometries and related them to geodesic and broken-ray transforms (Kenig and Salo 2013) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[KenigSalo2013](#ref-KenigSalo2013)].

Recent full-data results establish rigidity near prescribed smooth backgrounds. Lin proves rigidity near the Euclidean metric under smallness in a Hölder norm (Lin 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Lin2026](#ref-Lin2026)]. Muñoz-Thon and Stefanov prove local rigidity near every smooth conformally Euclidean metric under Sobolev smallness (Muñoz-Thon and Stefanov 2026, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Stefanov2026](#ref-Stefanov2026)]. Chen, Jiang, Liu, and Tao obtain same-patch results under quasianalyticity, and smooth results with additional foliation and one-sided ordering assumptions (Chen et al. 2026, Corollary 1.2, Theorem 1.6 and Corollary 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ChenJiangLiuTao2026](#ref-ChenJiangLiuTao2026)]. Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a> allows arbitrary smooth metrics and an arbitrary common input and observation patch.

Uhlmann and Wang recover compactly supported potentials, and conformal factors equal to one near the boundary, on a fixed near-Euclidean three-dimensional metric with strictly convex boundary and an invertible Dirichlet problem (Uhlmann and Wang 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[UhlmannWangNear2026](#ref-UhlmannWangNear2026)]. Their partial-data result recovers a potential near the measured boundary in a strictly convex three-dimensional domain, with the potential supported away from the measured patch (Uhlmann and Wang 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[UhlmannWangPartial2026](#ref-UhlmannWangPartial2026)]. Daudé, Enciso, Helffer, Kamran, and Nicoleau propagate local DN equality to a whole connected boundary component when the metrics already agree in a collar of that component (Daudé et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[DaudeEtAlPropagation2026](#ref-DaudeEtAlPropagation2026)]. These results clarify the separate roles of a prescribed background, an initial region of coefficient agreement, and the region reached by the measurements.

Harmonic functions provide an intrinsic way to describe points and coordinates. Greene and Wu studied embeddings by harmonic functions (Greene and Wu 1975) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GreeneWu1975](#ref-GreeneWu1975)]; the Poisson embedding approach of Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo represents a point by its evaluation on boundary-generated harmonic functions (Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo 2020) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[LLS20](#ref-LLS20)]. Their source embedding treats solutions generated in a remote interior region (Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo 2020, sec. 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[LLS20](#ref-LLS20)]. The smooth separation, approximation and finite-jet arguments are local tools; their global continuation results in dimensions at least three require real analyticity. Here the smooth continuation step is supplied by Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a>; its uniform estimate on shrinking surfaces is the central analytic argument.

The approximation mechanism goes back to Lax and Malgrange (Lax 1956; Malgrange 1956) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lax1956](#ref-Lax1956), [Malgrange1956](#ref-Malgrange1956)]. Rüland and Salo developed its quantitative supported-boundary form (Rüland and Salo 2019) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[RulandSalo2019](#ref-RulandSalo2019)]. We use point-distribution duality to obtain just the finite harmonic jets needed for the local argument. Localized boundary determination and exterior data transfer have further precedents in (Alessandrini and Gaburro 2009; Alessandrini and Kim 2012) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[AlessandriniGaburro2009](#ref-AlessandriniGaburro2009), [AlessandriniKim2012](#ref-AlessandriniKim2012)].

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The global argument is organized around the local metric extension Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a>. Its input is a pair of interior harmonic coordinate charts in which the metrics, their Green kernels, and a finite family of paired harmonic functions already agree on one side of a smooth hypersurface. The first-side Hessians span all second derivatives allowed by the harmonic equation. The theorem extends metric equality across the hypersurface. The main task is to prove this continuation theorem for smooth coefficients.

First, the measured energy form supplies the interior data needed to start. Local boundary determination recovers the full boundary jets and permits an exterior cap on which the metrics agree. A variational comparison then makes the actual Dirichlet Green kernels agree on the cap. Harmonic functions generated by sources in a remote part of that cap separate interior points and supply all admissible second-order harmonic jets. They will identify the paired charts and the finite harmonic family required by the local theorem.

Inside these charts, a mixed Green kernel solves the first metric equation in its first variable and the second metric equation in its second variable. Product continuation constructs this kernel at fixed positive separation from the diagonal. Common-basis methods for this separately harmonic extension have antecedents in (Zériahi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Zeriahi1982](#ref-Zeriahi1982)]. Its flux on a small sphere centered at $y$ defines a functional $T_y$ that maps local first-metric harmonic functions to local second-metric harmonic functions. It sends each chosen first-side function to its paired second-side function and reproduces constants and coordinates: $T_y1=1$ and $T_yx^i=y^i$. Existence at each positive radius is not yet a bound as that radius tends to zero.

To compare conformal classes, we normalize the inverse metrics to have the same trace against a reference metric conformal to the first. The shrinking argument temporarily assumes that their difference, together with a fixed finite number of its derivatives, is small compared with the sphere radius $r$. Under this assumption, we construct the transfer functional and bound its representing density uniformly in $L^2$ over a fixed set of centers and their unit spheres. Exact reproduction of constants and coordinates cancels the affine Taylor polynomial of a harmonic function. Its remainder has size $O(r^2)$ on the sphere, so for a paired harmonic difference $V$ we obtain an $L^2$ bound for $V/r^2$. Finite-order interpolation then bounds the needed derivatives of $V$ by $Cr^{3/2}$. The spanning harmonic Hessians turn this into the same superlinear bound for the normalized metric discrepancy and its required derivatives. It is a strict improvement of the temporary bound at small radii.

There are two scales in this argument. A spatial dilation is chosen once to make the initial metric discrepancy small and to handle radii above a fixed threshold. The strict improvement then allows the radius parameter to tend to zero with that dilation held fixed. The radii vanish on a fixed neighborhood, forcing equality of the normalized inverse metrics there. This determines the metrics up to a conformal factor. The common harmonic coordinates force that factor to be constant when $n\ge3$, and agreement on the known side fixes it.

The uniform density estimate must control the full equation obtained by differentiating traces on the moving spheres. Comparing spherical harmonic degree-raising and degree-lowering operators gives a positive weighted estimate, using the tensor and spherical-harmonic framework that also appears in tensor tomography (Paternain, Salo, and Uhlmann 2015; Guillarmou et al. 2016) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[PSU15](#ref-PSU15), [GPSU16](#ref-GPSU16)]. The moving geometry contributes second-order terms, which cannot simply be treated as lower-order errors. A second comparison retains those terms and bounds their contribution by the positive quantities in the first estimate.

Finally, the source evaluations make the local identifications single-valued and prevent an interior point from escaping to the boundary. A ball in the matched region touching its frontier supplies the one-sided geometry of the local theorem. Applying that theorem eliminates every interior frontier. The resulting isometry extends smoothly through the boundary, and the original supported boundary data force it to fix all of $\Gamma$.

Section <a href="#data:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:section">2</a> constructs the common cap and harmonic tests. Section <a href="#cross:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cross:section">3</a> states the local extension theorem and constructs product continuations at fixed separation; Section <a href="#sec:balls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:balls">4</a> uses them to construct the sphere functionals. Section <a href="#sec:angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:angular">5</a> proves the coercive estimate and bounds the transfer density. Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">6</a> turns the resulting moment bound into local metric extension. Section <a href="#global:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:section">7</a> gives the global identification and the boundary conclusion. Section <a href="#scalar:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:section">8</a> proves the scalar conductivity consequence.

<a id="data:section"></a>
### Boundary data and a common interior region

We first convert the measurements into data that can be continued in the interior. Equality of the energy maps determines the full boundary jets on the measured patch. These jets allow us to attach the same exterior cap to both metrics. Sources in that cap then supply common Green data and the finite families of harmonic functions needed in the local proof.

We use $\Delta_g=\operatorname{div}_g\nabla_g$ and positive Riemannian densities throughout. In particular, no orientation is chosen. The Dirichlet realization of $L_g=-\Delta_g$ on a compact connected smooth manifold with nonempty boundary is invertible: its quadratic form on $H^1_0$ is coercive. Smooth sources and boundary values give solutions smooth up to the boundary. We use interior elliptic regularity and unique continuation for smooth scalar second-order elliptic operators with real positive principal part, including a metric Laplacian multiplied by a positive smooth function. Here unique continuation means that an existing solution which vanishes on a nonempty open subset of a connected domain vanishes throughout that domain; see (Koch and Tataru 2005, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[KochTataru2005](#ref-KochTataru2005)]. For a positive scalar elliptic principal symbol, the local noncharacteristic-surface condition applies at every smooth hypersurface; propagation through overlapping coordinate balls gives this open-set form of continuation.

We will also use its boundary version. If a smooth harmonic function has both zero value and zero normal derivative on an open boundary patch, extend the metric smoothly across that patch and the function by zero. Green’s formula shows that the extended function solves the equation distributionally: neither a value jump nor a flux jump remains. Elliptic regularity and interior unique continuation give vanishing in a neighborhood of the patch, and then in the connected interior.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The density and the boundary jets

The distinction between the measured energy operator and the ordinary unit-normal DN operator matters in the first step. The latter sends $f$ to $\partial_{\nu_g}u_f$; the former sends it to the density $\partial_{\nu_g}u_f\,dS_g$.

<a id="stmt-data-jets"></a>
<div id="data:jets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Boundary jets). *Let $g_1,g_2$ be smooth metrics on a compact connected smooth $n$-manifold $M$ with nonempty boundary, where $n\ge3$. Suppose their energy maps agree for inputs and observations in a nonempty relatively open set $\Gamma\subset\partial M$. In boundary-normal coordinates for the respective metrics, based on the same coordinates on $\Gamma$, their complete boundary Taylor jets agree.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-data-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-data-jets</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a boundary chart compactly contained in $\Gamma$, write $m=n-1\ge2$, and let $h$ denote the tangential metric matrix. Relative to the coordinate density, the energy operator has principal symbol $$e_1(z,\xi)=\sqrt{\det h(z)}
             \sqrt{h^{ab}(z)\xi_a\xi_b}.$$ Thus its square determines the positive matrix $$C=(\det h)h^{-1},\qquad
 \det C=(\det h)^{m-1},\qquad
 h=(\det C)^{1/(m-1)}C^{-1}.$$ The two induced metrics, and hence their boundary densities, are therefore equal. Dividing the common density-valued operator by this known density gives equality of the ordinary DN operators locally. Cutoffs supported in $\Gamma$ and equal to one near a chosen point give equality of their complete symbols there.

We give the scalar symbol recursion to make its smooth and local content explicit. This boundary determination goes back to Lee and Uhlmann (1989) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[LeeUhlmann1989](#ref-LeeUhlmann1989)]; see also Joshi and Lionheart (2005, Theorem 1.1(ii) and Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[JoshiLionheart2005](#ref-JoshiLionheart2005)]. In inward normal coordinates $s\ge0$, write $$g=ds^2+h(s,z),\qquad
 a=\frac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(h^{-1}\partial_s h),\qquad
 L_g=-\partial_s^2-a\partial_s+P,$$ where $P=-\Delta_{h(s)}$ is tangential. Smooth boundary factorization gives a tangential operator $B(s)$ of order one, with positive principal symbol, such that <a id="data:factorization"></a>$$\label{data:factorization}
 L_g=(-\partial_s+B-a)(\partial_s+B)
          \pmod{\Psi^{-\infty}},\qquad
 B^2-aB-\partial_sB=P\pmod{\Psi^{-\infty}}.$$ Here the remainders are tangentially smoothing and depend smoothly on $s$. The local Poisson parametrix identifies $B(0)$, modulo smoothing, with the outward DN operator: the decaying branch satisfies $(\partial_s+B)u=0$ and $\partial_\nu=-\partial_s$. This is a local factorization for smooth coefficients; it makes no analyticity assumption.

<a id="data:highest-jet"></a>
For precision, use left symbols and $D_z=-i\partial_z$, so $$b\mathbin{\#}c\sim
 \sum_\alpha\frac{(1/i)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}
       (\partial_\xi^\alpha b)(\partial_z^\alpha c).$$ Write $b\sim b_1+b_0+b_{-1}+\cdots$ for the symbol of $B$ and $p=p_2+p_1$ for that of $P$. Then $$b_1=\rho=(h^{ab}\xi_a\xi_b)^{1/2},\qquad
 b\#b-ab-\partial_sb=p.$$ The equation of degree one is $$2\rho b_0+\frac1i\partial_\xi\rho\cdot\partial_z\rho
             -a\rho-\partial_s\rho=p_1.$$ The terms $p_1$ and the tangential composition term involve only $h$ and its tangential derivatives. Since $$\partial_s\rho
 =-\frac{(\partial_s h)(\xi^\sharp,\xi^\sharp)}{2\rho},$$ the part of $b_0$ containing a normal derivative is $$\frac14\left(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h\partial_s h
       -\frac{(\partial_s h)(\xi^\sharp,\xi^\sharp)}{\rho^2}\right).$$ At each subsequent degree the new coefficient is obtained from $$2\rho b_{1-k}=\partial_s b_{2-k}+R_k,\qquad k\ge2,$$ where $R_k$ uses normal derivatives of $h$ only through order $k-1$ and finitely many tangential derivatives. Indeed all other terms in the symbol product use earlier coefficients, and tangential differentiation does not increase normal derivative order. Writing $A_k=\partial_s^k h$, induction gives the highest-jet term $$\label{data:highest-jet}
 b_{1-k}=
 \frac{1}{4(2\rho)^{k-1}}
 \left(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h A_k-
        \frac{A_k(\xi^\sharp,\xi^\sharp)}{\rho^2}\right)
 +\text{terms involving lower normal jets}.$$ Differentiating $\rho$, the raising of indices, or the coefficient in the preceding induction step contributes only lower-jet terms.

Suppose the two normal jets agree through order $k-1$ as smooth functions on the boundary chart. Their tangential derivatives also agree. Equality of the symbols and Equation <a href="#data:highest-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="data:highest-jet">[data:highest-jet]</a> give, for $A=\partial_s^k(h_1-h_2)|_{s=0}$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h A-A(v,v)=0\qquad(|v|_h=1).$$ Polarization yields $A=(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h A)h$. Taking the trace gives $(m-1)\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_h A=0$, so $A=0$. Starting from the already recovered boundary metric, induction proves all normal and mixed jet equalities. The normal metric components are $g_{ss}=1$ and $g_{sa}=0$, so these are the full metric jets.

Every step is local to a half-coordinate ball and uses densities. Although the differential-form formulation in (Joshi and Lionheart 2005) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[JoshiLionheart2005](#ref-JoshiLionheart2005)] assumes orientability, this scalar coordinate proof requires none. Other boundary components affect only the local smoothing remainder. Finally, equality of smooth jets asserts no equality in an interior collar. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Attaching a common cap

The jets provide a common region on the exterior side of a smaller patch. The following energy argument transfers the measured data to that region without making assumptions on the inaccessible boundary.

<a id="stmt-data-cap"></a>
<div id="data:cap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Common cap and Green data). *Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#data:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jets">4</a>, there are compact connected smooth extensions $(M_j^e,g_j^e)$ of $(M,g_j)$ and an identified connected open exterior cap $E$ on which the extended metrics agree. The cap is attached through a nonempty open set $P_0\Subset\Gamma$. The extended zero-Dirichlet Green kernels satisfy $$G_1(x,y)=G_2(x,y),\qquad x,y\in E,\quad x\ne y.$$ For every $F\in C_c^\infty(E)$, the two extended zero-Dirichlet solutions with source $F$ agree on $E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-data-cap"><summary>Proof of stmt-data-cap</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a boundary coordinate patch $P\Subset\Gamma$ and a nonnegative smooth bump $b$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}b\Subset P$ and connected, nonempty positivity set $P_0=\{b>0\}$. Use the separate inward normal collars of Lemma <a href="#data:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jets">4</a>, and replace the boundary $s=0$ by the graph $s=-b(z)$. Take $b$ sufficiently small that this graph lies in a collar. It gives smooth compact connected extended manifolds. Their common open added part is $$E=\{(z,s):-b(z)<s<0\}.$$ Extend the first metric smoothly to negative $s$ and use the same exterior metric for the second. A smooth extension of its coordinate coefficients exists in a collar and remains positive definite after shortening the collar. Their jets match on an open neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}b$. Pasting across the entire hypersurface $s=0$ in that neighborhood is therefore smooth, to every order. Restricting this pasting to $s\ge-b(z)$ proves smoothness also at the edges of the bump. We henceforth omit the superscript on the extended metrics.

For the following comparison, identify the two extensions by the identity on the original $M$ and by the common collar coordinates on $E$. This identification and its inverse are smooth up to each side of the seam, with bounded derivatives, and agree on the seam. In collar charts they are therefore bi-Lipschitz. In particular they identify the spaces $H^1_0$ on the two extensions.

Fix a real $F\in C_c^\infty(E)$ and let $w_j$ minimize $$\mathcal J_j(w)=\frac12\int_{M_j^e}|dw|_{g_j}^2\,dV_{g_j}
                       -\int_E Fw\,dV_{g_j}
       \quad\text{on }H^1_0(M_j^e).$$ The minimizers are smooth up to the extended boundary and solve $-\Delta_{g_j}w_j=F$. Their traces $h_j$ on the original boundary are smooth and supported in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}b\Subset\Gamma$: wherever $b=0$, that boundary is also the new zero-data boundary. Transport $w_1$ by this identification and replace it inside the original manifold by the $g_2$-harmonic extension of $h_1$, leaving it unchanged on $E$. The difference between the replacement and the transported $w_1$ is the zero extension of a function in $H^1_0(M)$; thus the replacement is an admissible competitor in $H^1_0(M_2^e)$. This argument does not require the narrowing cap to have a regular boundary at its edges.

The interior energies before and after replacement are the equal quadratic energies of $h_1$ for the two measured maps. The cap metric and source pairing are common. Hence $\min\mathcal J_2\le\min\mathcal J_1$. Reversing the roles gives equality. Strict convexity of the Dirichlet form makes the replacement equal to $w_2$, proving $w_1=w_2$ on $E$.

Normalize the symmetric Dirichlet Green kernels by <a id="data:green-normalization"></a>$$\label{data:green-normalization}
 -\Delta_{g_j,x}G_j(x,y)=\delta_y^{g_j}(x),\qquad
 G_j(\,\cdot\,,y)|_{\partial M_j^e}=0,
 \qquad
 w_j(x)=\int_{M_j^e}G_j(x,y)F(y)\,dV_{g_j}(y),$$ where the point mass has unit mass relative to metric volume. The source densities are identical on $E$. Varying $F$ gives equality of the distribution kernels on $E\times E$, and therefore pointwise equality off the diagonal. ◻

</details>

Fix a nonempty open source set $U_0\Subset E$. Figure <a href="#data:cap-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:cap-figure">1</a> shows the two boundaries and a possible choice of this set.

<figure id="data:cap-figure">

<figcaption>A local schematic cross-section of the common exterior cap. The boundary moves only over a compact subset of the measured patch. The oval is a nonempty open source set $U_{0}$ with closure inside $E$. The proof uses the smooth graph, including its flat bump edges.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-data-cap-figure"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`data:cap-figure`)

 \fill[gray!12] (-3,-1.25) rectangle (3,0);
 \fill[blue!12] (-2,0) .. controls (-1.5,0) and (-1.3,1.0) .. (0,1.0)
                 .. controls (1.3,1.0) and (1.5,0) .. (2,0) -- cycle;
 \draw[thick] (-3,0)--(3,0);
 \draw[thick,blue!60!black] (-2,0)
                 .. controls (-1.5,0) and (-1.3,1.0) .. (0,1.0)
                 .. controls (1.3,1.0) and (1.5,0) .. (2,0);
 \draw[dashed] (-2,-0.13)--(-2,0.23);
 \draw[dashed] (2,-0.13)--(2,0.23);
 \node at (0,-0.7) {$M$};
 \node at (-1,0.38) {$E$};
 \draw[fill=white] (0.55,0.44) ellipse (0.34 and 0.19);
 \node at (0.55,0.44) {$U_0$};
