<a id="sec:angular"></a>
### An estimate on the shrinking spheres

The functional in Proposition <a href="#balls:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:functional">15</a> is defined at every positive radius. We now estimate its representing density uniformly as the radius shrinks. The equation for this density has second-order angular and base terms. Comparing a degree-raising operator with a degree-lowering operator will control both kinds of derivatives; a second comparison will retain the principal terms caused by the changing metric.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The equation for the density

<a id="angular:profile-bounds"></a>
We use the reference metric $\gamma$, the normalized second principal matrix $Q$, and $h=Q-\gamma^{-1}$ from <a href="#balls:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:normalization">[balls:normalization]</a>. In this section the subscript $\delta$ on the positive radius $r$ is suppressed. Put $$f=\log r,\qquad \beta=|df|_\gamma.$$ The required profile bounds are $$\label{angular:profile-bounds}
 \beta\ge\beta_0,\qquad
 \operatorname{Hess}_\gamma f\le-c_0\beta\gamma,
 \qquad |\nabla^j f|\le C_j\beta^j\quad(1\le j\le8),$$ where $c_0>0$ and $C_j$ are fixed. Lemma <a href="#balls:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:profiles">13</a> provides these bounds with $\beta_0$ arbitrarily large by choosing $p$ sufficiently large. All background metric bounds below are on a fixed relatively compact coordinate region; their constants are uniform in the spatial dilation.

Let $S_\gamma Y$ be the bundle of $\gamma$-unit tangent spheres over the base region $Y$. Each sphere has probability measure $d\sigma_y$. Horizontal differentiation $\nabla$ uses parallel transport for $\gamma$, including the covariant tensor indices of an operator or section. This connection preserves the sphere measure. Unless otherwise specified, all norms and full adjoints use $dV_\gamma(y)\,d\sigma_y(\omega)$.

<a id="angular:elementary-identities"></a>
On each tangent unit ball, extend a sphere function harmonically for the Euclidean metric $\gamma(y)$. Denote the boundary radial derivative of this extension by $B$, and its ambient derivatives in an orthonormal frame by $A_i$. Thus $B=k$ on the space $\mathcal H_k$ of spherical harmonics of degree $k$, and $A_i:\mathcal H_k\to\mathcal H_{k-1}$. The star in $A_i^*$ denotes the sphere adjoint. These operators are parallel, with the indicated tensor indices, and $$\label{angular:elementary-identities}
 [B,A_i^*]=A_i^*,\qquad \sum_i A_i^*A_i^*=0.$$ Indeed differentiation lowers homogeneous degree by one, and the second identity is the adjoint of the vanishing Laplacian of a harmonic extension. For $s\in\mathbb R$, use the fiber Sobolev norm $$\|v(y,\cdot)\|_s=\|(1+B)^s v(y,\cdot)\|_{L^2(S_y)}.$$ It is equivalent to the usual Sobolev norm on the unit sphere. Tensor-valued versions use the sum over an orthonormal frame.

We write $\Psi^m$ for the classical pseudodifferential operators of order $m$ on the sphere. For a positive function $a(y)$, the notation $a\Psi^m$ means that, after division by $a$, the finitely many symbol and operator seminorms used below are bounded. A statement with $j$ base derivatives permits the indicated additional factor $\beta^j$.

<a id="stmt-angular-trace"></a>
<div id="angular:trace" class="lemma">

<a id="angular:smallness"></a>
<a id="angular:C-expansion"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Moving trace operators). *Suppose that the normal balls and the functional $T_y$ of Proposition <a href="#balls:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:functional">15</a> are defined, and that $$\label{angular:smallness}
 \sum_{|\alpha|\le8}|\partial_y^\alpha h|\le\varepsilon r,
 \qquad r\beta^2\le\varepsilon.$$ If $u$ is first-metric harmonic near one such ball, set $$U(y,\omega)=u\bigl(\exp_y^\gamma(r(y)\omega)\bigr).$$ There are sphere operators $\mathcal C_i$ such that $\nabla_iU=\mathcal C_iU$. Their sphere adjoints satisfy $$\label{angular:C-expansion}
 \mathcal C_i^*=r^{-1}A_i^*+f_iB+E_i+F_i,
 \qquad E_i\in r\Psi^1,\quad F_i\in\Psi^0,
 \qquad f_i=\nabla_i f.$$ The normalized remainders $r^{-1}E_i$ and $F_i$ have uniform symbol bounds after up to six base derivatives, with the allowance $C_j\beta^j$.*

<a id="angular:P-equation"></a>
*Write the normalized second equation, in the reference connection, as $Q^{ij}\nabla_i\nabla_j+b_2^i\nabla_i$. Its drift $b_2$ is uniformly controlled. If $\psi(y,\omega)$ represents $T_y$ against $d\sigma_y$, then $$\label{angular:P-equation}
 P\psi=0,\qquad
 P=r\left[Q^{ij}(\nabla_i+\mathcal C_i^*)
                    (\nabla_j+\mathcal C_j^*)
             +b_2^i(\nabla_i+\mathcal C_i^*)\right].$$ The products in this formula are covariant products. In particular, $Q$ is not differentiated by taking a base adjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-angular-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-angular-trace</summary>

<a id="angular:radial-expansion"></a>
*Proof.* Normalize the principal part of the first equation to $\gamma^{-1}$ and pull it back by $z\mapsto\exp_y^\gamma(rz)$ to the unit ball. After multiplication by $r^2$, its principal coefficients are $\delta^{ij}+O(r^2)$ and its first-order coefficients are $O(r)$. The resulting smooth family remains uniformly elliptic and has an invertible Dirichlet problem, including at $r=0$. Its radial derivative operator therefore has the expansion $$\label{angular:radial-expansion}
 B+r B_1(y)+r^2 B_2(y,r),\qquad
 B_1\in\Psi^0,\quad B_2\in\Psi^1.$$ Here is the parameter justification. The classical boundary-symbol construction is described in (Joshi and Lionheart 2005, Theorem 1.1(i) and Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[JoshiLionheart2005](#ref-JoshiLionheart2005)]; we keep the center and radius dependence explicitly. In boundary coordinates the decaying normal root of the principal symbol constructs the Dirichlet parametrix. Successive lower-order transport equations construct its symbols smoothly in $(y,r)$. The first normal derivative trace is an operator of order one, whose principal symbol depends only on the principal coefficients and the differentiating vector. Those coefficients have zero first $r$-derivative at $r=0$, so that derivative of the trace operator has order zero. Taylor’s formula in this symbol class gives <a href="#angular:radial-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:radial-expansion">[angular:radial-expansion]</a> to smoothing error. The true solution differs from the parametrix by the Dirichlet inverse of a smooth error; differentiated Dirichlet estimates control this remainder in every fixed seminorm. More explicitly, on a compact center set the rescaled Dirichlet operator is smoothly invertible between each fixed pair of Sobolev spaces, uniformly for $0\le r\le r_0$. Differentiating that inverse controls every fixed number of parameter derivatives of the smoothing correction; increasing the Sobolev orders controls its smooth kernel seminorms. Thus the Taylor expansion holds in the complete classical symbol topology, including the smoothing remainder, and not merely in one Sobolev operator norm. This proves the stated expansion and its parameter bounds.

Differentiate the exponential map in its center, transporting $\omega$ parallel to the center velocity. The Jacobi field has initial derivative zero. After inverting the differential in the $z$ variable, the velocity acting on the unit-ball solution is $$r^{-1}\bigl(e_i+O(r^2)\bigr)+f_i\omega.$$ The coefficient error is smooth in $(y,r,\omega)$. Tangential derivatives act directly on the boundary value; the radial derivative is <a href="#angular:radial-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:radial-expansion">[angular:radial-expansion]</a>. At $r=0$ the first term is $r^{-1}A_i$. The order-one errors are $O(r)$, and the order-zero errors are bounded: in particular $r f_i B_1$ is bounded because $r\beta\le\varepsilon/\beta$. Taking sphere adjoints gives <a href="#angular:C-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:C-expansion">[angular:C-expansion]</a>. Differentiating the radius costs at most one factor $\beta$ per derivative, by <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a>; the asserted parameter estimates follow.

For fixed $u$, differentiate $T_yu=\langle\psi(y,\cdot),U(y,\cdot)\rangle_{S_y}$. Metric compatibility and $\nabla_iU=\mathcal C_iU$ put the differentiated operator on $\psi$ as $\nabla_i+\mathcal C_i^*$. The local second-metric harmonicity in Proposition <a href="#balls:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:functional">15</a> proves that <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a> pairs to zero with the trace of every function harmonic near the fixed closed ball. No global extension or source-family approximation is used in this step. At a fixed sphere these traces are dense among smooth boundary data: solve the smooth Dirichlet problem on slightly enlarged balls, using boundary values transported from the limiting sphere, and use smooth dependence of the solution on the radius. Thus the paired equation is the stated equation for $\psi$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The model operator and its principal perturbations

We separate a degree-raising model operator from the remaining principal and lower-order terms of the moving trace equation. We continue under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#angular:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:trace">16</a>, including <a href="#angular:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:smallness">[angular:smallness]</a>.

<a id="angular:P-decomposition"></a>
<a id="angular:U-bounds"></a>
<a id="angular:real-principal-part"></a>
Define <a id="angular:T-definition"></a>$$\label{angular:T-definition}
 D_i^+=\nabla_i+f_iB,\qquad D_i^-=-\nabla_i+f_iB,
 \qquad
 T=\sum_i A_i^*D_i^+,\quad T^\flat=\sum_i A_iD_i^-.$$ The symbol $T$ here denotes a differential operator on sphere functions; the transfer functional continues to be denoted $T_y$. Expand <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a> by <a href="#angular:C-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:C-expansion">[angular:C-expansion]</a>. The identities <a href="#angular:elementary-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:elementary-identities">[angular:elementary-identities]</a> give $$D_i^+(r^{-1}A_j^*)=r^{-1}A_j^*D_i^+,
 \qquad \sum_iA_i^*A_i^*=0.$$ Thus, in ordinary base coordinates and an orthonormal sphere trivialization, $$\label{angular:P-decomposition}
 P=2T+U+J,$$ where $J$ is a uniformly bounded family in $\Psi^1$, with no base derivatives, and $$\label{angular:U-bounds}
 U=\sum_{|\alpha|\le2}U_\alpha(y)\partial_y^\alpha,
 \qquad U_\alpha\in\varepsilon\beta^{-|\alpha|}
                               \Psi^{2-|\alpha|}.$$ Up to five derivatives of these coefficients have the additional allowance $C_j\beta^j$. The second-base-derivative term is precisely $$\label{angular:real-principal-part}
 rQ^{ij}\partial_{y_i}\partial_{y_j};$$ its coefficients are real scalars independent of the sphere variable.

We detail these bounds. The pure angular second derivative contracted with $\gamma^{-1}$ vanishes. The remaining contraction is $(h^{ij}/r)A_i^*A_j^*$, and belongs to the zero-base-derivative term of <a href="#angular:U-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:U-bounds">[angular:U-bounds]</a>. The mixed correction is $2h^{ij}A_i^*D_j^+$ and has the stated small bounds. The $D^+D^+$ term has coefficient $r$; its base orders two, one and zero have coefficients $O(r)$, $O(r\beta)$ and $O(r\beta^2)$, respectively. The condition $r\beta^2\le\varepsilon$ gives <a href="#angular:U-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:U-bounds">[angular:U-bounds]</a> for all three. Terms containing $E_i$ obey the same bounds. Terms containing $F_i$ also do so, except for their cross terms with $r^{-1}A_j^*$, which are bounded angular order-one terms and belong to $J$. The drift term with $A_i^*$ belongs to $J$ as well. Differentiating any of these expressions gives the stated coefficient bounds by <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a> and <a href="#angular:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:smallness">[angular:smallness]</a>. A horizontal connection written in this trivialization adds a bounded angular first derivative, which preserves the same estimates. The bounds for $J$ use the fixed background and remain uniform as $\beta_0$ is increased.

The decomposition identifies the model operator $2T$ and the second-order perturbation $U$. To carry the model estimate over to $P$ we shall need more than an ordinary first-derivative estimate: the error forms also contain mixed base and angular derivatives. For a sphere function $\varphi$, put <a id="angular:N-definition"></a>$$\label{angular:N-definition}
 \mathcal N(\varphi)^2
 =\int_Y\left(
  \|\nabla\varphi\|_0^2+\beta^2\|\varphi\|_1^2
  +\beta^{-1}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{1/2}^2
  +\beta\|\varphi\|_{3/2}^2\right)dV_\gamma.$$

The last two terms of $\mathcal N$ place half an angular derivative on $\nabla\varphi$ and three halves on $\varphi$. They will bound the remaining error forms left by the comparison with $P$.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### A comparison of raising and lowering operators

The next estimate supplies these two derivative strengths for the model operator. Spherical harmonics are trace-free symmetric tensors, and comparing their raising and lowering operators is familiar from energy identities in tensor tomography; see, for example, (Paternain, Salo, and Uhlmann 2015; Guillarmou et al. 2016) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[PSU15](#ref-PSU15), [GPSU16](#ref-GPSU16)]. Here the negative Hessian of the spatial weight supplies the positive term that absorbs the bounded curvature. We give the calculation with its degree factors.

<a id="stmt-angular-raising"></a>
<div id="angular:raising" class="lemma">

<a id="angular:raising-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Weighted raising estimate). *Let $n\ge3$, and suppose <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a> holds on a fixed base region with uniformly bounded reference geometry. There are $\beta_0$ and $c>0$, depending only on that geometry and the constants in <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a>, such that every smooth, compactly base-supported function with zero fiber mean satisfies $$\label{angular:raising-estimate}
 \|T\varphi\|^2-\|T^\flat\varphi\|^2
       \ge c\,\mathcal N(\varphi)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-angular-raising"><summary>Proof of stmt-angular-raising</summary>

*Proof.* Both operators change angular degree by exactly one, so it suffices to prove the estimate at one input degree $k\ge1$. Identify that component with a harmonic homogeneous polynomial $v(z)$ of degree $k$. Use the Fischer inner product, in which the monomials $z^\alpha$ are orthogonal with squared norm $\alpha!$. Polynomial differentiation $a_i=\partial_{z_i}$ is adjoint to multiplication $M_i=z_i$. On harmonic polynomials of degree $k$ the Fischer squared norm is the normalized squared sphere norm multiplied by $$N_k=n(n+2)\cdots(n+2k-2),\qquad N_0=1.$$ For completeness, integration against a standard real Gaussian identifies the Fischer norm on trace-free homogeneous polynomials with their Gaussian $L^2$ norm; integrating radially then gives the factor $N_k$. These classical identities are also recorded in (Render 2008, Equation (2.1) and Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Render08](#ref-Render08)].

<a id="angular:Fock-difference"></a>
Set $d=k+n/2-1$ and use $D_i^\pm=\pm\nabla_i+k f_i$ at this fixed degree. For harmonic polynomials $q_i$ of degree $k$, the Laplacian of $\sum_iM_iq_i$ is $2\sum_i a_iq_i$. Since $$\Delta_z(|z|^2s)=4d\,s\qquad(s\in\mathcal H_{k-1}),$$ its harmonic projection is $\sum_iM_iq_i-|z|^2\sum_i a_iq_i/(2d)$. Orthogonality of this projection and its complement shows that its squared norm is $$\sum_i\|q_i\|^2+\sum_{i,j}(a_jq_i,a_iq_j)
             -d^{-1}\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iq_i\Bigr\|^2.$$ Changing from Fischer adjoints to sphere adjoints gives $$A_i^*|_{\mathcal H_k}
   =\frac{N_{k+1}}{N_k}\Pi_{k+1}M_i,$$ where $\Pi_{k+1}$ is harmonic projection in homogeneous degree $k+1$. Consequently $N_k$ times the left side of <a href="#angular:raising-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:raising-estimate">[angular:raising-estimate]</a> is $$\label{angular:Fock-difference}
 \begin{split}
 (2d+2)\left[\|D^+v\|^2
   +\sum_{i,j}(a_jD_i^+v,a_iD_j^+v)
   -\frac1d\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^+v\Bigr\|^2\right]
 -2d\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^-v\Bigr\|^2.
 \end{split}$$ All norms in this calculation include integration over the base and use the polynomial inner product in the fibers. The factors are $N_{k+1}/N_k=2d+2$ and $N_k/N_{k-1}=2d$.

<a id="angular:ibp-one"></a>
<a id="angular:ibp-two"></a>
<a id="angular:ibp-three"></a>
Write $C_{ij}=2k(\operatorname{Hess}_\gamma f)_{ij}$ and $$C[av]=\int_Y\sum_{i,j}C_{ij}\langle a_iv,a_jv\rangle\,dV_\gamma.$$ Covariant integrations by parts give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i,j}(a_jD_i^+v,a_iD_j^+v)
   &=\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^-v\Bigr\|^2-C[av]
                                    +O(k^2)\|v\|^2,\label{angular:ibp-one}\\
 \Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^+v\Bigr\|^2
   &\le k\|D^-v\|^2-C[av]+O(k^2)\|v\|^2,\label{angular:ibp-two}\\
 \|D^-v\|^2
   &=\|D^+v\|^2+\int_Y\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C\,|v|^2\,dV_\gamma.
                                                    \label{angular:ibp-three}
\end{aligned}$$ Here and below a form denoted $O(k^2)\|v\|^2$ has absolute value at most a fixed constant times that quantity. To verify the first two formulas, move a $D_j^+$ across the pairing and commute $D_j^-D_i^+$ to $D_i^+D_j^--C_{ij}$. The reordered term in <a href="#angular:ibp-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:ibp-two">[angular:ibp-two]</a> is at most $k\|D^-v\|^2$, because $\sum_i|a_iw|^2=k|w|^2$ for $w\in\mathcal H_k$. The extra commutator is curvature: it acts as a sum of rotations of norm $O(k)$ at degree $k$, and the two contractions supply one more factor $k$. This proves the claimed error bounds. Expanding the two squares proves <a href="#angular:ibp-three" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:ibp-three">[angular:ibp-three]</a>. For complex functions all the displayed forms are understood through their real parts.

<a id="angular:after-first-ibp"></a>
<a id="angular:convex-bound"></a>
Substituting <a href="#angular:ibp-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:ibp-one">[angular:ibp-one]</a> into <a href="#angular:Fock-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:Fock-difference">[angular:Fock-difference]</a> rewrites that expression as $$\label{angular:after-first-ibp}
 \begin{split}
 &(2+2/d)\left[d\|D^+v\|^2
       -\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^+v\Bigr\|^2-dC[av]\right]\\
 &\qquad+2\Bigl\|\sum_i a_iD_i^-v\Bigr\|^2
                      +O(k^3)\|v\|^2.
 \end{split}$$ We must control the remaining negative divergence square while retaining both an ordinary derivative estimate and an additional half-order angular derivative estimate. The latter requires a coefficient of size $k/\beta$ in front of $|D^+v|^2$, including when $k$ is much larger than $\beta$. Choose a small constant $\vartheta>0$. On the fraction $1-\vartheta$ of its negative divergence square use <a href="#angular:ibp-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:ibp-two">[angular:ibp-two]</a>–<a href="#angular:ibp-three" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:ibp-three">[angular:ibp-three]</a>. On the remaining fraction use the pointwise convex combination $$\label{angular:convex-bound}
 \begin{split}
 \Bigl|\sum_i a_iD_i^+v\Bigr|^2
 &\le(1-\zeta)(k+n-1)|D^+v|^2\\
 &\quad+\zeta\left(2\Bigl|\sum_i a_iD_i^-v\Bigr|^2
                         +8k^3\beta^2|v|^2\right),
 \qquad \zeta=c_1/\beta.
 \end{split}$$ The fraction $1-\vartheta$ in the integrated estimates is constant; the $y$-dependent $\zeta$ is used only in this pointwise inequality. The first bound follows from the adjoint multiplication operators, since the contraction $(q_i)\mapsto\sum_i a_iq_i$ has squared norm at most $k+n-1$ on polynomials of degree $k$. The second follows from $D_i^++D_i^-=2k f_i$ and $|\sum_i f_i a_i v|^2\le k\beta^2|v|^2$.

Combining these bounds in <a href="#angular:after-first-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:after-first-ibp">[angular:after-first-ibp]</a>, the coefficient retained for $|D^+v|^2$ is <a id="angular:positive-degree-factor"></a>$$\label{angular:positive-degree-factor}
 (2+2/d)\bigl[d-k-\vartheta(n-1)
                         +\vartheta\zeta(k+n-1)\bigr].$$ Since $d-k=(n-2)/2$, a sufficiently small fixed $\vartheta$ makes this at least a fixed positive constant plus a positive multiple of $c_1k/\beta$. The coefficient of $|\sum_i a_iD_i^-v|^2$ is nonnegative when $\beta_0$ is sufficiently large. The Hessian contribution is exactly <a id="angular:hessian-contribution"></a>$$\label{angular:hessian-contribution}
 -(2+2/d)\left[(d-1+\vartheta)C[av]
       +(1-\vartheta)k\int_Y\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C\,|v|^2\,dV_\gamma\right].$$ For $k\ge1$ and $n\ge3$, both coefficients inside this expression are positive. The Hessian hypothesis bounds it below by $c k^3\int\beta|v|^2$. This absorbs the curvature error $O(k^3)\|v\|^2$ by increasing $\beta_0$, and absorbs the last term of <a href="#angular:convex-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:convex-bound">[angular:convex-bound]</a> by choosing $c_1$ sufficiently small. We have therefore retained positive multiples of $$\|D^+v\|^2,\qquad
 \int_Y\frac{k}{\beta}|D^+v|^2\,dV_\gamma,
 \qquad k^3\int_Y\beta|v|^2\,dV_\gamma.$$ The first controls both $\|\nabla v\|^2$ and $k^2\int\beta^2|v|^2$, since its cross term is $-k\int\Delta_\gamma f\,|v|^2\ge0$. For the weighted derivative use the pointwise inequality $$\frac{k}{\beta}|\nabla v|^2
 \le2\frac{k}{\beta}|D^+v|^2+2k^3\beta|v|^2.$$ No differentiation of $\beta^{-1}$ is required. Dividing by $N_k$, and then summing the orthogonal degree components, proves <a href="#angular:raising-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:raising-estimate">[angular:raising-estimate]</a>; the weights $(1+k)^s$ are comparable to $k^s$ because the zero degree is excluded. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Keeping the principal perturbations

The raising estimate controls the model operator. The actual equation <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a> also contains second base derivatives and a small second-order angular perturbation. We retain these terms in a comparison of squared norms.

<a id="stmt-angular-coercivity"></a>
<div id="angular:coercivity" class="proposition">

<a id="angular:coercive-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Coercivity for the moving trace equation). *Fix a base region with bounded reference geometry and the constants in <a href="#angular:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:profile-bounds">[angular:profile-bounds]</a>. Suppose $P$ is the operator <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a>, with the trace operators of Lemma <a href="#angular:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:trace">16</a>. There exist $\beta_0$ sufficiently large, $\varepsilon_0>0$ and $C<\infty$, depending only on these fixed bounds, such that <a href="#angular:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:smallness">[angular:smallness]</a> with $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$ implies $$\label{angular:coercive-estimate}
 \mathcal N(\varphi)\le C\|P\varphi\|$$ for every smooth, compactly base-supported function $\varphi$ with zero mean on each sphere. These constants are independent of the shrinking parameter. Only the eight indicated derivatives of the small tensor $h$ are required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-angular-coercivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-angular-coercivity</summary>

<a id="angular:norm-comparison"></a>
<a id="angular:error-operator"></a>
<a id="angular:error-form-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Let a dagger denote the full adjoint for $dV_\gamma d\sigma$. Covariant integration and the degree shift give <a id="angular:Z-definition"></a>$$\label{angular:Z-definition}
 Z:=T^\flat-T^\dagger=\sum_i f_iA_i.$$ A direct expansion yields $$\label{angular:norm-comparison}
 \begin{split}
 &\|(2T+U)\varphi\|^2-
                \|(2T^\flat+U^\dagger)\varphi\|^2\\
 &\qquad=4\bigl(\|T\varphi\|^2-\|T^\flat\varphi\|^2\bigr)
                       +\langle\mathcal E\varphi,\varphi\rangle,
 \end{split}$$ where $$\label{angular:error-operator}
 \mathcal E=2[T^\dagger,U]+2[U^\dagger,T]+[U^\dagger,U]
                       -2\bigl(UZ+(UZ)^\dagger\bigr).$$ We will prove $$\label{angular:error-form-bound}
 |\langle\mathcal E\varphi,\varphi\rangle|
                         \le C\varepsilon\mathcal N(\varphi)^2.$$

Here are the complete order and weight counts needed for this claim. An operator has count $(m,s)$ if its coefficient at $\partial_y^\alpha$ is angular order $m-|\alpha|$, of size $O(\beta^{s-|\alpha|})$, with the derivative allowances already specified. Adjoints preserve the count, and products add the counts. When a base derivative hits a coefficient it consumes one differential order and adds a factor $\beta$, so it preserves the weight index. A commutator of scalar angular operators loses one angular order: the products of their principal symbols cancel. Hence a scalar commutator loses one in total order while preserving the sum of the weight indices. The counts of $T,T^\dagger,U,Z$ are, respectively, $$(2,1),\quad(2,1),\quad(2,0),\quad(1,1),$$ and every occurrence of $U$ carries the factor $\varepsilon$. Thus <a href="#angular:error-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:error-operator">[angular:error-operator]</a> has count at most $(3,1)$ and a factor $O(\varepsilon)$; the quadratic $U$ term is smaller.

<a id="angular:error-types"></a>
There is a further cancellation that the total count alone does not express: no third base derivative survives. To check it explicitly, use an orthonormal sphere trivialization and write the base measure as $\mu(y)\,dy$. The sphere probability measure is then fixed. Write $$U=a^{ij}\partial_i\partial_j+\mathcal B^i\partial_i+\mathcal C,
 \qquad a^{ij}=rQ^{ij}=a^{ji}\in\mathbb R.$$ The coefficients $\mathcal B^i$ and $\mathcal C$ are angular operators. Replacing a horizontal derivative by a coordinate derivative plus its angular connection term changes only base orders at most one, so these are all the second-base coefficients. If a star denotes the sphere adjoint, direct integration by parts gives $$\begin{aligned}
 U^\dagger-U
 ={}&\left(2\mu^{-1}\partial_j(\mu a^{ij})
                -\mathcal B^i-(\mathcal B^i)^*\right)\partial_i\\
 &+\mu^{-1}\partial_i\partial_j(\mu a^{ij})
       -\mu^{-1}\partial_i\bigl(\mu(\mathcal B^i)^*\bigr)
       +\mathcal C^*-\mathcal C.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular it has base order at most one. In $[T^\dagger,U]$ and its partner, the possible third-base-derivative coefficient is a commutator of an angular coefficient with $rQ^{ij}$, which is zero by <a href="#angular:real-principal-part" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:real-principal-part">[angular:real-principal-part]</a>. For $[U^\dagger,U]$, first write it as $[U^\dagger-U,U]$. The operator $U^\dagger-U$ has no second base derivative, since the coefficient in <a href="#angular:real-principal-part" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:real-principal-part">[angular:real-principal-part]</a> is real. Taking adjoints against the smooth base density only produces lower base orders. Its remaining possible third-base-derivative coefficients commute for the same reason. Finally, $UZ$ already has at most two base derivatives. It follows that $\mathcal E$ is a sum of terms of precisely the following three permitted types: $$\label{angular:error-types}
 \varepsilon\beta^{-1}\Psi^1\partial_y^2,
 \qquad \varepsilon\Psi^2\partial_y,
 \qquad \varepsilon\beta\Psi^3.$$ This argument also covers lower orders, since $\beta\ge1$.

For the first type in <a href="#angular:error-types" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:error-types">[angular:error-types]</a>, integrate once in the base. The principal form is bounded by $C\varepsilon\int\beta^{-1}\|\partial_y\varphi\|_{1/2}^2$. A derivative of its coefficient costs one factor $\beta$ and leaves an $\varepsilon\Psi^1\partial_y$ term. This includes differentiation of the weight itself: $|d\beta|\le C\beta^2$ implies $|d(\beta^{-1})|\le C$. The second type has the form bound $$C\varepsilon\int_Y
     \|\partial_y\varphi\|_{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{3/2}\,dV_\gamma
 \le C\varepsilon\int_Y\left(
     \beta^{-1}\|\partial_y\varphi\|_{1/2}^2
                 +\beta\|\varphi\|_{3/2}^2\right)dV_\gamma.$$ The third type is bounded by $C\varepsilon\int\beta\|\varphi\|_{3/2}^2$. The lower-order term from differentiating a coefficient satisfies the same bound. Replacing coordinate derivatives by horizontal derivatives adds a bounded angular first derivative, again controlled by these norms. A fixed finite partition of the base region has the same harmless lower-order errors. This proves <a href="#angular:error-form-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:error-form-bound">[angular:error-form-bound]</a>.

The constants in the preceding estimates are uniform for all $\beta\ge\beta_0$ once a fixed lower threshold is met. Choose $\varepsilon_0$ small relative to the constant in Lemma <a href="#angular:raising" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:raising">17</a>. Dropping the nonnegative second squared norm in <a href="#angular:norm-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:norm-comparison">[angular:norm-comparison]</a> gives $\mathcal N(\varphi)\le C\|(2T+U)\varphi\|$. Finally, $$\|J\varphi\|\le C\|\varphi\|_{L^2(Y;H^1(S_y))}
                         \le C\beta_0^{-1}\mathcal N(\varphi),$$ so a sufficiently large $\beta_0$ absorbs $J$ and proves <a href="#angular:coercive-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:coercive-estimate">[angular:coercive-estimate]</a>.

All these operations use angular symbol estimates pointwise in the base, and ordinary base differential operators of degree at most two. Formal adjoints, the commutator expansion and the one integration by parts use at most the five base coefficient derivatives specified in <a href="#angular:U-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:U-bounds">[angular:U-bounds]</a>. Construction of these coefficients differentiates the moving trace operators once and the second metric to only finitely many orders below eight. Higher angular symbol seminorms depend on the fixed smooth first-metric family; differentiating in the angular variable does not differentiate the center-dependent tensor $h$. Thus the eight smallness derivatives in <a href="#angular:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:smallness">[angular:smallness]</a> suffice in every fixed dimension. No smallness assumption on all derivatives of $h$ is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The constant mode and the uniform density bound

We now apply the coercive estimate to the transfer density constructed in Section <a href="#sec:balls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:balls">4</a>, restoring the notation $r=r_\delta$. Its normalization is $\int_{S_y}\psi\,d\sigma_y=1$, so $\chi(\psi-1)$ has zero fiber mean for the fixed cutoff <a href="#balls:cutoff-region" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:cutoff-region">[balls:cutoff-region]</a>. This is the mean-zero condition required by Proposition <a href="#angular:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:coercivity">18</a>.

The decomposition <a href="#angular:P-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-decomposition">[angular:P-decomposition]</a> gives a uniform bound for $P1$: its raising part annihilates $1$, only $U$’s bounded zero-base-derivative coefficient acts on $1$, and $J$ is uniformly bounded in $\Psi^1$. There is no factor growing with $\beta$ in this assertion.

<a id="angular:cutoff-bound"></a>
For any fixed smooth base cutoff, the same decomposition gives $$\label{angular:cutoff-bound}
 \|[P,\chi]w\|
 \le C_\chi\left(
  \|w\|_{L^2(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}d\chi;H^1(S_y))}
    +\|r\nabla w\|_{L^2(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}d\chi\times S_y)}\right),$$ where the support can be replaced by a fixed small neighborhood of the supports of all derivatives of $\chi$. Indeed $[2T,\chi]=2\sum_iA_i^*(\nabla_i\chi)$, the second base derivative of $U$ contributes $2rQ^{ij}(\partial_i\chi)\partial_j$ and a bounded zeroth-base-derivative term, and its first base derivative contributes a bounded angular order-one term. The operator $J$ commutes with $\chi$.

<a id="stmt-local-density"></a>
<div id="local:density" class="lemma">

<a id="local:density-bound"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Uniform transfer density). *Fix the structural parameters so that Propositions <a href="#balls:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:continuation">14</a> and <a href="#angular:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:coercivity">18</a> apply. There is a constant $C$ independent of $\delta$ and of sufficiently small spatial dilations $\lambda$ such that $$\label{local:density-bound}
 \|\chi\psi\|_{L^2(S_\gamma Y)}\leq C$$ whenever the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a> holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-density</summary>

<a id="local:density-equation"></a>
*Proof.* Set $\varphi=\chi(\psi-1)$, which is compactly supported in the base and has zero fiber mean. For the actual moving-sphere operator $P$, Equation <a href="#angular:P-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:P-equation">[angular:P-equation]</a> gives $$\label{local:density-equation}
 P\varphi=P\bigl(\chi(\psi-1)\bigr)
       =-\chi P1+[P,\chi](\psi-1).$$ The cutoff estimate <a href="#angular:cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="angular:cutoff-bound">[angular:cutoff-bound]</a> involves only angular derivatives through order one and $r\nabla\psi$ on the supports of derivatives of $\chi$. Proposition <a href="#balls:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:functional">15</a> bounds these there uniformly: on the lower region the density is that of ordinary evaluation, and on the other region the radii stay at a fixed positive separation. Together with the bound for $P1$, this gives a uniformly bounded $L^2$ norm for the right side of <a href="#local:density-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:density-equation">[local:density-equation]</a>. Proposition <a href="#angular:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:coercivity">18</a> bounds $\varphi$; adding the fixed function $\chi$ proves <a href="#local:density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:density-bound">[local:density-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

The uniform bound now applies to the density representing the actual Green functional at every radius allowed by <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a>. Exact affine moments will turn it into a two-power bound for the paired harmonic differences.

<a id="sec:bootstrap"></a>
### From harmonic differences to local metric extension

Lemma <a href="#local:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:density">19</a> bounds the transfer density uniformly on the shrinking spheres. Exact reproduction of affine functions now turns that bound into a two-power estimate for the difference of paired harmonic functions. The harmonic Hessians convert derivatives of these differences into metric derivatives. Finite-order interpolation will then improve the temporary metric bound and allow the radii to shrink with the spatial dilation held fixed.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### From the functional to harmonic jets

Return to the finite harmonic family in Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a>. Its values and first derivatives at $0$ agree between the two metrics, because the functions agree on the one-sided open set and are smooth. After dilation define <a id="local:renormalized-pairs"></a>$$\label{local:renormalized-pairs}
 v_{j,\lambda}^a(y)=\lambda^{-2}
 \bigl(u_j^a(\lambda y)-u_1^a(0)
                     -\lambda\,du_1^a(0)\cdot y\bigr).$$ The subtracted functions are harmonic because the coordinates are harmonic. These new pairs still agree on the known side and are transferred by $T_y$. For each fixed integer $m$ they have uniform $C^m$ bounds on every required fixed bounded coordinate neighborhood. For derivative orders $j\geq2$, this follows from the factor $\lambda^{j-2}$; orders zero and one follow from the second-order Taylor remainder. Moreover, $$\partial^2v_{1,\lambda}^a(y)\longrightarrow\partial^2u_1^a(0)$$ smoothly on fixed sets. Thus the Hessian spanning condition has a uniform rank bound after sufficiently small dilation. Write $V^a=v_{2,\lambda}^a-v_{1,\lambda}^a$.

<a id="stmt-local-moment"></a>
<div id="local:moment" class="lemma">

<a id="local:moment-bound"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Two-power moment bound). *Under the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a>, the paired differences satisfy $$\label{local:moment-bound}
 \left\|\frac{\chi V^a}{r_\delta^2}\right\|_{L^2(Y)}\leq C$$ with a constant independent of $\delta$ and sufficiently small $\lambda$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-moment</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a center $y$ and subtract from $v_{1,\lambda}^a$ its affine coordinate Taylor polynomial at $y$. Exact constant and coordinate reproduction in <a href="#balls:reproduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:reproduction">[balls:reproduction]</a> cancels this polynomial in $T_yv_{1,\lambda}^a-v_{1,\lambda}^a(y)=V^a(y)$. The remainder on $\partial B_\gamma(y,r)$ is bounded by $Cr^2$, since coordinate distance and $\gamma$-distance are uniformly comparable and the functions have uniform second derivatives. Hence $$|V^a(y)|\leq C r_\delta(y)^2
                         \|\psi(y,\cdot)\|_{L^2(S_y)}.$$ Lemma <a href="#local:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:density">19</a> proves <a href="#local:moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:moment-bound">[local:moment-bound]</a>. The coordinate volume measure and $dV_\gamma$ are uniformly comparable. No derivative in $y$ of the continued density is used here. ◻

</details>

<a id="local:metric-system"></a>
We next express metric derivatives in terms of derivatives of the paired differences. Recovery of the conformal metric class from the hyperplane of harmonic Hessians is also used in (Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo 2020, Proposition 4.1 and Remark 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[LLS20](#ref-LLS20)]. All Hessians in this step are ordinary coordinate Hessians, not covariant Hessians. Because the coordinates are harmonic, the equations for $v_{j,\lambda}^a$ have no first-order term in these coordinates: $$(g_{k,\lambda}^{-1})^{ij}\partial_{ij}v_{k,\lambda}^a=0
 \qquad(k=1,2).$$ Multiplication by the scalar factors in <a href="#balls:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:normalization">[balls:normalization]</a> therefore gives $$\label{local:metric-system}
 h^{ij}\partial_{ij}v_{1,\lambda}^a
       =-Q^{ij}\partial_{ij}V^a,
 \qquad \operatorname{tr}(\gamma h)=0.$$

<a id="local:metric-jet-bound"></a>
Harmonicity puts each $\partial^2v_{1,\lambda}^a(y)$ in the hyperplane annihilated by $\gamma^{-1}(y)$. The uniform rank bound established above shows that these matrices span this hyperplane for small $\lambda$. The extra equation $\operatorname{tr}(\gamma h)=0$ completes an injective system on symmetric contravariant matrices: an element annihilating all those Hessians is a multiple of $\gamma^{-1}$, and its $\gamma$-trace is $n$ times that multiple. The associated finite matrix has a smooth left inverse with uniform derivatives on $Y$. For example, in fixed coordinate bases use $(A^*A)^{-1}A^*$, where $A$ is this injective matrix. Its smallest singular value stays bounded below by the spanning condition and smooth convergence under dilation. Differentiating <a href="#local:metric-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:metric-system">[local:metric-system]</a> at most eight times gives $$\label{local:metric-jet-bound}
 H_8(y)\leq C\sum_a\sum_{|\alpha|\leq10}
                              |\partial^\alpha V^a(y)|.$$ The derivatives of $Q$, $\gamma$, and of the finite harmonic family are uniformly bounded. In particular, this inversion introduces no factor involving $r_\delta^{-1}$.

The metric estimate requires derivatives of the paired differences through order ten, whereas Lemma <a href="#local:moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:moment">20</a> controls only their weighted $L^2$ norm. The following finite-order interpolation estimate bridges these two statements using the already fixed smooth bounds.

<a id="stmt-local-interpolation"></a>
<div id="local:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Finite-order interpolation). *Let $V$ be smooth on a neighborhood of $\overline{B(y,s)}\subset
\mathbb R^n$, with $0<s\leq1$ and $\|V\|_{C^m}\leq A_m$ there. For every multi-index $\alpha$ of length $j<m$, <a id="local:interpolation-estimate"></a>$$\label{local:interpolation-estimate}
 |\partial^\alpha V(y)|\leq C_{m,n}
 \bigl(s^{-j-n/2}\|V\|_{L^2(B(y,s))}
                          +A_m s^{m-j}\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $V(y+sz)$ at $z=0$ through degree $m-1$ on the unit ball. The remainder has absolute value at most $C_{m,n}A_m s^m$. The $L^2$ norm of the Taylor polynomial is therefore at most $s^{-n/2}\|V\|_{L^2(B(y,s))}+C_{m,n}A_m s^m$. On the finite-dimensional space of polynomials of degree at most $m-1$, every coefficient is bounded by a constant times the $L^2$ norm on the unit ball. Its $z^\alpha$ coefficient is $s^j\partial^\alpha V(y)/\alpha!$, giving the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### A strict improvement of the metric bound

<a id="local:exponents"></a>
Set $$\label{local:exponents}
 D=10+n/2,\qquad \eta=\frac1{2D},\qquad
 p>2D,\qquad m\geq\lceil10+3D\rceil.$$ We also take the integer $p$ large enough for Proposition <a href="#angular:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="angular:coercivity">18</a>. Thus $\eta>1/p$, and $m$ is one fixed finite smoothness order. The constants may depend on these choices, which precede the final spatial dilation and all shrinkage.

<a id="stmt-local-improvement"></a>
<div id="local:improvement" class="lemma">

<a id="local:superlinear"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Superlinear improvement). *After the choices in <a href="#local:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:exponents">[local:exponents]</a>, there are $C>0$ and $\rho_0>0$, independent of $\delta$ and sufficiently small $\lambda$, such that the coefficient condition <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a> implies $$\label{local:superlinear}
 H_8(y)\leq C r_\delta(y)^{3/2}
 \quad\text{for }y\in K\text{ with }r_\delta(y)<\rho_0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-improvement"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-improvement</summary>

*Proof.* Put $r=r_\delta(y)$ and $s=r^\eta$. By Lemma <a href="#balls:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:profiles">13</a>, $r_\delta^{1/p}$ has a fixed Lipschitz constant. Since $$\frac{s}{r^{1/p}}=r^{\eta-1/p}\longrightarrow0,$$ a sufficiently small threshold $\rho_0$ ensures $$\tfrac12 r^{1/p}\leq r_\delta(z)^{1/p}
                         \leq\tfrac32 r^{1/p}
             \quad(z\in B(y,s)).$$ The same fixed threshold ensures that this ball lies where $\chi=1$, because $K$ has a fixed buffer inside that set. The upper comparison and Lemma <a href="#local:moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:moment">20</a> imply $$\|V^a\|_{L^2(B(y,s))}\leq C r^2.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#local:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:interpolation">21</a> and the fixed uniform $C^m$ bounds of the renormalized functions. For $j\leq10$ it gives $$|\partial^\alpha V^a(y)|
   \leq C_m\bigl(r^{2-\eta(j+n/2)}+r^{\eta(m-j)}\bigr)
   \leq C_m r^{3/2}.$$ Both exponents are at least $3/2$ by <a href="#local:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:exponents">[local:exponents]</a>. Equation <a href="#local:metric-jet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:metric-jet-bound">[local:metric-jet-bound]</a> completes the proof. ◻

</details>

The gain in <a href="#local:superlinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:superlinear">[local:superlinear]</a> is stronger than the temporary bound $H_8\leq\varepsilon r_\delta$. Its usefulness is that a single choice of the spatial dilation makes the improvement strict at every radius, including those above the small threshold.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Local extension of the isometry

<details id="proof-stmt-local-improvement-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-improvement</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a>.* Make the linear normalization and spatial dilation of Section <a href="#sec:balls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:balls">4</a>. All harmonic functions and their finite spanning family are chosen before the dilation. We now specify the remaining choices and hold each one fixed before making the next.

First choose the fixed conformal geometry, cylinder, depth function, and cutoff, keeping uniform background smooth bounds for $0<\lambda\leq\lambda_0$. Choose $p,\eta,m$ as in <a href="#local:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:exponents">[local:exponents]</a>, with $p$ large enough for the geometric and angular estimates. Choose $\varepsilon$ for those estimates and then $R_*$ so that the balls lie in normal neighborhoods and $r_\delta\beta_\delta^2\leq\varepsilon$ for every $\delta$. These choices fix every separation range needed to start the continuation and to bound the cutoff terms. Restrict $\lambda_0$ further so that Proposition <a href="#cross:initial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cross:initial">12</a> applies to these fixed ranges, with bounds uniform for $0<\lambda\leq\lambda_0$.

<a id="local:rho-choice"></a>
<a id="local:one-dilation"></a>
The constants $C,\rho_0$ of Lemma <a href="#local:improvement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:improvement">22</a> are now fixed. Choose $$\label{local:rho-choice}
 0<\rho<\rho_0,\qquad C\rho^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon/2.$$ At points with $r_\delta<\rho$, that lemma improves the coefficient bound to $H_8\leq\varepsilon r_\delta/2$ whenever the temporary bound holds. At points with $r_\delta\geq\rho$, the same improvement will hold if $$\label{local:one-dilation}
 \sup_Y H_8\leq\varepsilon\rho/2.$$ Since $h\to0$ smoothly under dilation, choose one $\lambda\leq\lambda_0$ satisfying <a href="#local:one-dilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:one-dilation">[local:one-dilation]</a> and the additional initial bounds described next. This $\lambda$ is kept fixed for the rest of the proof.

There is a uniform reason the coefficient bound holds off $K$ for every $\delta$. Outside $K$, either $y_n<-1/2$, where $h=0$ after the fixed dilation is sufficiently small, or $t>2t_b$, where $$r_\delta\geq R_*(2t_b)^p>0.$$ Smooth smallness of $h$ therefore gives $H_8\leq\varepsilon r_\delta/2$ off $K$ for every $\delta$ by reducing the same dilation once. At $\delta=1$, the radius has a positive minimum on all of $Y$, so we can also ensure the initial coefficient bound there. These conditions involve only fixed positive constants, and are compatible with <a href="#local:one-dilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:one-dilation">[local:one-dilation]</a>.

For this fixed metric pair on the dilated neighborhood, set $$\mathcal A=\{\delta\in(0,1]:
        H_8(y)\leq\varepsilon r_\delta(y)\text{ for every }y\in K\}.$$ It contains $1$. At any $\delta\in\mathcal A$, the bound already established off $K$ gives the full hypothesis <a href="#balls:bootstrap-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:bootstrap-condition">[balls:bootstrap-condition]</a>. Proposition <a href="#balls:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="balls:continuation">14</a> and the uniform density estimate of Lemma <a href="#local:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:density">19</a> then apply. The moment bound, metric system, and interpolation argument give Lemma <a href="#local:improvement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:improvement">22</a>. The small-radius improvement <a href="#local:rho-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rho-choice">[local:rho-choice]</a> and the large-radius bound <a href="#local:one-dilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:one-dilation">[local:one-dilation]</a> give $$H_8\leq\tfrac12\varepsilon r_\delta\quad\text{on }K.$$ For every fixed positive $\delta$, $r_\delta$ has a positive minimum on the compact set $K$. Thus $\delta\mapsto\max_K(H_8/r_\delta)$ is continuous on $(0,1]$. The set $\mathcal A$ is relatively closed, and the strict improvement makes it relatively open. Connectedness implies $\mathcal A=(0,1]$.

Finally let $\delta\downarrow0$, with the spatial dilation, harmonic family, cutoff and all structural parameters fixed. On the fixed open neighborhood of $0$ where $t<0$, the radii tend to zero. Hence $h=0$ there. By <a href="#balls:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="balls:normalization">[balls:normalization]</a>, $g_{2,\lambda}$ is conformal to $g_{1,\lambda}$ on that neighborhood. Write $g_{2,\lambda}=e^{2\omega}g_{1,\lambda}$. The conformal change formula for the Laplace–Beltrami operator is $$\Delta_{e^{2\omega}g}u
  =e^{-2\omega}\bigl(\Delta_g u
                 +(n-2)\langle d\omega,du\rangle_g\bigr).$$ Apply it to every common harmonic coordinate. Since their differentials form a basis and $n-2\ne0$, it forces $d\omega=0$. Since $g_{1,\lambda}(0)=g_{2,\lambda}(0)=I$, this constant is zero. This is the local harmonic-morphism rigidity mechanism of (Fuglede 1978, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Fuglede1978](#ref-Fuglede1978)], in the restricted-family form used in (Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo 2020, Proposition 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[LLS20](#ref-LLS20)]. The metrics therefore agree on a connected neighborhood of $0$; undoing the dilation proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

For clarity, the two limits in this proof have different roles: spatial dilation is used once to obtain a fixed small metric difference, and the subsequent shrinking family improves the bound for that same difference. The parameter order is <a id="local:parameter-order"></a>$$\label{local:parameter-order}
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{fixed geometry and harmonic family}
   \;\longrightarrow\;(p,\eta,m)
   \;\longrightarrow\;(\varepsilon,R_*)\\
 \longrightarrow\;\rho\;\longrightarrow\;
 \lambda\text{ fixed}\;\longrightarrow\;\delta\downarrow0.
 \end{gathered}$$ All estimates use finitely many derivatives selected before either limiting step. Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a> is the local input that will prevent a maximal matched region from having an interior frontier.

<a id="global:section"></a>
### The global isometry and the measured boundary

We now apply Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a> to the data constructed in Section <a href="#data:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:section">2</a>. The source evaluations identify which points should correspond. Their separation makes this correspondence single-valued, and their behavior at the boundary prevents its escape from the interior. The local theorem then removes every interior frontier of the isometric correspondence. The use of a maximal matching set and limits of source evaluations follows the source-embedding architecture in (Lassas, Liimatainen, and Salo 2020, sec. 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[LLS20](#ref-LLS20)]; Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a> supplies the smooth continuation step needed here.

<a id="global:matching"></a>
Retain the extended manifolds $M_j^e$, their common cap $E$, the open source set $U_0\Subset E$, and the functionals $I_j$ of Equation <a href="#data:evaluations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="data:evaluations">[data:evaluations]</a>. For this section an *admissible match* is a smooth local isometry $$\Phi:W\longrightarrow (M_1^e)^\circ,
 \qquad W\subset(M_2^e)^\circ,$$ where $W$ is connected, open, and contains $E$, such that $$\label{global:matching}
 \Phi|_E=\mathrm{Id},\qquad I_2(p)=I_1(\Phi(p))\quad(p\in W).$$ Thus $\Phi^*g_1=g_2$ on $W$. The identity on $E$ is an admissible match by Proposition <a href="#data:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:cap">5</a>.

<a id="stmt-global-frontier"></a>
<div id="global:frontier" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (No interior frontier). *There is an isometry $$\Phi:(M_2^e)^\circ\longrightarrow(M_1^e)^\circ$$ onto the first interior satisfying Equation <a href="#global:matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:matching">[global:matching]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-frontier"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-frontier</summary>

*Proof.* *The maximal match and its Green kernel.* Two admissible matches agree wherever their domains overlap, since $I_1$ separates interior points by Lemma <a href="#data:jet-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jet-family">6</a>. Their union therefore defines a single smooth local isometry on the union $W$ of all admissible domains. This union is connected because every domain contains the connected set $E$. The union map is injective: equal images give equal $I_2$ evaluations, hence equal original points. It is itself an admissible match and is maximal by construction.

<a id="global:green-equality"></a>
We first record the Green equality on this larger region: $$\label{global:green-equality}
 G_2(p,q)=G_1(\Phi(p),\Phi(q)),
             \qquad p,q\in W,\quad p\ne q.$$ For fixed $p\in W$, equality of its evaluations and the common source density give this equality for the other variable in $U_0$, initially in the distributional sense. Both Green functions are smooth away from their pole. After pullback by the local isometry, their difference is harmonic on $W\setminus\{p\}$. This set is connected, and equality holds on a nonempty open part of it. Unique continuation proves Equation <a href="#global:green-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:green-equality">[global:green-equality]</a>.

*A frontier partner and common coordinates.* Suppose $W$ is not the entire second interior. Connectedness gives an interior frontier point $p$. If $p_k\in W$ tends to $p$, compactness of $M_1^e$ gives a subsequential limit $q$ of $\Phi(p_k)$. For every fixed source $f$, continuity up to the boundary gives $$I_2(p)(f)=I_1(q)(f).$$ The point $q$ is interior: all boundary evaluations are zero, whereas $I_2(p)$ is nonzero. Any two such limits have the same evaluations and so coincide. It follows that every matched sequence tending to $p$ has partners tending to this unique $q$. In particular, nearby matched points have partners in any prescribed neighborhood of $q$.

Both $p$ and $q$ are outside $\overline{U_0}$. The first statement holds because $\overline{U_0}\subset E\subset W$. If $q$ belonged to $\overline{U_0}$, the common-cap identification would give $I_1(q)=I_2(q)$; separation on the second manifold would force $p=q\in E$, a contradiction.

Choose sources $f_1,\ldots,f_n$ so that $$y=(u_{f_1}^2,\ldots,u_{f_n}^2)$$ has independent differentials at $p$, and put $x=(u_{f_1}^1,\ldots,u_{f_n}^1)$ near $q$. The second tuple is also a coordinate system. Indeed the isometries at the points $p_k$ give equality of the gradient Gram matrices $$\bigl(\langle du_{f_a}^2,du_{f_b}^2\rangle_{g_2}(p_k)\bigr)_{a,b}
 =
 \bigl(\langle du_{f_a}^1,du_{f_b}^1\rangle_{g_1}(\Phi(p_k))\bigr)_{a,b}.$$ The first limit is positive definite, and so is the second. The coordinate values at $p,q$ coincide. Restricting the charts gives a common coordinate ball $V$ about this value, with closures away from $\overline{U_0}$. The preceding convergence observation ensures that every already matched point sufficiently near $p$ has its partner in the first chart. In these coordinates its identification is $x=y$.

Let $A\subset V$ be the coordinate image of the already matched second-side points. It is open and has the center value in its relative frontier. The two coordinate metrics and every paired source function agree on $A$. By Lemma <a href="#data:jet-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jet-family">6</a>, choose finitely many first-side source functions whose ordinary Hessians span the hyperplane annihilated by $g_1^{-1}$ at the center. After shrinking $V$, their spanning rank has a positive lower bound throughout $V$. Their second-side partners are harmonic there and agree with them on $A$.

*A touching ball removes the frontier.* We can now find a smooth ball touching the unknown part, without assuming any regularity of the frontier of $A$. Choose $c\in A$ sufficiently close to the center that $$r=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(c,V\setminus A)>0,\qquad
 \overline{B(c,r)}\Subset V.$$ For example, if the center has distance $R$ from $\partial V$, take $c$ within $R/3$ of it. Then $r<R/3$, and a minimizing sequence stays in a compact subset of $V$. Thus the distance is attained at some $$z_*\in\partial B(c,r)\cap(V\setminus A).$$ On $B(c,r)$ the metrics, the finite harmonic pairs, and the Green kernels in both variables agree. Smoothness gives equality of the metric and harmonic-function jets at $z_*$, and the Hessian spanning condition holds there by our choice of $V$. All the inputs of Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a> are therefore satisfied at $z_*$. It gives equality of the coordinate metrics on a neighborhood of $z_*$. We may take a small connected ball there meeting $B(c,r)$. Each paired source difference is now harmonic for the common metric and vanishes on that intersection. Unique continuation makes every pair agree on the same ball. The resulting local isometry thus matches all evaluations.

This isometry agrees with the existing one on every overlap, by separation of evaluations. Its union with $W$ is an admissible match whose domain contains $z_*$, contradicting maximality. Consequently $W=(M_2^e)^\circ$.

*Surjectivity.* The image is open because $\Phi$ is a local diffeomorphism. It is also closed in the first interior. Indeed, if $\Phi(p_k)$ tends to an interior point $q$, a subsequence of $p_k$ converges in the compact second manifold to $p$. Evaluation continuity again gives $I_2(p)=I_1(q)$, so $p$ cannot lie on the boundary. Then $p$ is in the domain and $\Phi(p)=q$. Connectedness of the first interior makes the image all of it. An injective surjective local diffeomorphism has a smooth inverse, proving the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Returning to the original manifold

The interior isometry still has two duties: it must extend smoothly through the original boundary, and it must fix every point of the measured patch, including components where no cap was attached.

<a id="stmt-global-completion"></a>
<div id="global:completion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Smooth completion and the accessible gauge). *The isometry of Proposition <a href="#global:frontier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frontier">23</a> extends to a smooth diffeomorphism $M_2^e\to M_1^e$. It preserves the original copies of $M$, and its restriction satisfies $$g_2=\Phi^*g_1,\qquad \Phi|_\Gamma=\mathrm{Id}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-completion</summary>

*Proof.* An interior isometry preserves lengths of curves and hence the intrinsic distances. The metric completion of the interior of a compact smooth Riemannian manifold with boundary is the manifold itself with its length metric. To see the only boundary issue, use a collar to push a boundary-touching curve an arbitrarily small distance into the interior; its length changes arbitrarily little. Compactness and local metric comparison then identify the completion with the original compact manifold. Thus $\Phi$ and its inverse extend to mutually inverse homeomorphisms of the two completions. Boundary is mapped to boundary, since the interiors already correspond bijectively.

These extensions are smooth. In a sufficiently small uniform boundary collar, the distance $s$ to the boundary is a smooth function, and its unit gradient is the inward normal flow. The isometry preserves $s$ and, in the interior, intertwines its gradients. Fix a small positive level $s=s_0$. The map is already smooth on that interior hypersurface. Expressing points with $0\le s\le s_0$ by normal flow from this level expresses $\Phi$ as the composition of this smooth level map with smooth normal flows on both sides. This extends smoothly to $s=0$. The same argument applies to the inverse.

Since $\Phi$ is the identity on $E$, continuity makes it the identity on $\overline E$. The original interiors satisfy $$M_j^\circ=(M_j^e)^\circ\setminus\overline E.$$ The extended map preserves these complements, and hence their closures, the original manifolds. Its restriction is therefore a smooth diffeomorphism $\Phi:M\to M$ with $g_2=\Phi^*g_1$. It fixes the attached original patch $P_0=\{b>0\}$ pointwise.

To prove the full boundary assertion, take any $f\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma)$ and let $v_f^j$ now denote the harmonic functions on the *original* manifolds with boundary value $f$. On the second manifold both $v_f^2$ and $v_f^1\circ\Phi$ are $g_2$-harmonic. Their difference has zero boundary value on $P_0$, where $\Phi$ is the identity. It also has zero normal derivative there. In fact the equal energy maps give $$(\partial_{\nu_1}v_f^1)\,dS_{g_1}
 =(\partial_{\nu_2}v_f^2)\,dS_{g_2}
                    \quad\text{on }P_0.$$ The boundary densities are the same by Lemma <a href="#data:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jets">4</a>, and the smooth isometry transports the outward unit normal. Boundary unique continuation, followed by interior continuation, therefore gives $$v_f^2=v_f^1\circ\Phi\quad\text{throughout }M.$$ Taking boundary traces yields $$f(q)=f(\Phi(q))\qquad
       (q\in\partial M,\ f\in C_c^\infty(\Gamma)).$$ For $q\in\Gamma$, these test functions distinguish $q$ from every other boundary point: choose a function equal to one at $q$ with support in a small neighborhood avoiding the other point. Thus $\Phi(q)=q$ for all $q\in\Gamma$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-completion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-completion</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a>.* Lemma <a href="#data:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jets">4</a>, Proposition <a href="#data:cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:cap">5</a>, and Lemma <a href="#data:jet-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="data:jet-family">6</a> provide the common cap, Green kernels, and finite harmonic tests. Theorem <a href="#local:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:extension">8</a> applies at every interior contact in Proposition <a href="#global:frontier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frontier">23</a>. Proposition <a href="#global:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:completion">24</a> gives the required diffeomorphism. All integrations use densities; the argument required neither an orientation nor connectedness of the boundary or of $\Gamma$. ◻

</details>

<a id="scalar:section"></a>
### Scalar conductivities on a fixed Euclidean domain

The geometric theorem determines a metric up to a change of coordinates. For scalar conductivities on a fixed Euclidean domain, the corresponding change of coordinates is conformal. We first show that fixing a boundary patch removes this remaining freedom, then verify the exact conversion between the two measurement maps.

<a id="stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity"></a>
<div id="scalar:boundary-rigidity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Conformal rigidity at a boundary patch). *Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a connected open set with smooth boundary, where $n\ge3$. Suppose $F:\overline\Omega\to\overline\Omega$ is a smooth diffeomorphism through the boundary and $$F^*e=e^{2b}e\quad\text{in }\Omega$$ for a real $b\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$, where $e$ is the Euclidean metric. If $F$ fixes a nonempty relatively open subset of $\partial\Omega$ pointwise, then $F=\mathrm{Id}$ and $b=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity</summary>

*Proof.* The differential identities below are the classical local mechanism behind Liouville’s conformal rigidity theorem; see Kushelman and McGrath (2024, Equations (4)–(6)) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[KushelmanMcGrath2024](#ref-KushelmanMcGrath2024)]. We include the argument with its boundary initial values, since preservation of the interior side is essential here.

<a id="scalar:connection"></a>
The Christoffel symbols of $e^{2b}e$ are $$\Gamma^k_{ij}=b_i\delta^k_j+b_j\delta^k_i-b_k\delta_{ij},
 \qquad b_i=\partial_i b.$$ Since $F$ is an isometry from this metric to the Euclidean metric, the connection transformation rule gives $$\label{scalar:connection}
 D^2F(v,w)
 =DF\bigl(db(v)w+db(w)v-\langle v,w\rangle\nabla b\bigr).$$ This identity initially holds in the interior and extends to the boundary by smoothness.

Fix a point $q$ of the fixed boundary patch and let $\nu$ be the Euclidean inward unit normal there. For every tangent vector $v$, differentiating $F=\mathrm{Id}$ along the fixed patch gives $DF(q)v=v$. Conformality therefore implies $b(q)=0$ and makes $DF(q)$ orthogonal. It follows that $DF(q)\nu$ is either $\nu$ or $-\nu$. To determine the sign, choose a smooth local defining function $s$ positive in $\Omega$, with $ds(q)\nu=1$. For small $a>0$, both $q+a\nu$ and $F(q+a\nu)$ are interior points. Hence the right derivative at zero of $s(F(q+a\nu))$ is nonnegative. It is $ds(q)DF(q)\nu=\pm1$, so the positive sign holds. Consequently $DF(q)=I$ on the entire fixed patch.

The tangential derivatives of $b$ vanish there because $b=0$. Choose a smooth curve $c$ in the patch with $c(0)=q$ and a nonzero tangent $v=c'(0)$. Twice differentiating $F(c(a))=c(a)$ gives $$D^2F(q)(v,v)+DF(q)c''(0)=c''(0),$$ so $D^2F(q)(v,v)=0$. Equation <a href="#scalar:connection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:connection">[scalar:connection]</a>, together with $DF(q)=I$ and $db(q)v=0$, now yields $|v|^2\nabla b(q)=0$. Thus both $b$ and $\nabla b$ vanish on the fixed patch. In particular, the argument rules out a conformal map that fixes a hypersurface but exchanges its two sides.

The metric $e^{2b}e=F^*e$ is flat. Substitution of the displayed Christoffel symbols into its Ricci tensor gives $$0=\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}
 =-(n-2)(b_{ij}-b_i b_j)
   -\bigl(\Delta b+(n-2)|\nabla b|^2\bigr)\delta_{ij}.$$ Taking the Euclidean trace and substituting back, using $n-2\ne0$, gives <a id="scalar:hessian"></a>$$\label{scalar:hessian}
 b_{ij}=b_i b_j-\tfrac12|\nabla b|^2\delta_{ij}.$$ Along a straight segment of constant velocity $v$, the vector $q_b=\nabla b$ therefore solves $$q_b'=(q_b\cdot v)q_b-\tfrac12|q_b|^2v.$$ Start with a short inward segment at a point of the fixed patch. Its initial value is zero, and the right side of this ordinary differential equation is locally Lipschitz in $q_b$. Uniqueness implies $q_b=0$ along that segment. Every connected open subset of Euclidean space is polygonally connected: the set reachable from one point by polygonal paths is both open and relatively closed, because small balls lie in the domain. Apply the same uniqueness argument successively along those segments to conclude $\nabla b=0$ throughout $\Omega$. Continuity at the initial boundary point gives $b=0$.

Equation <a href="#scalar:connection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:connection">[scalar:connection]</a> now gives $D^2F=0$ in the connected domain, so $F$ is affine there. Its derivative and value at the fixed boundary point are those of the identity. Thus $F=\mathrm{Id}$ in $\Omega$ and on its closure by continuity. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-boundary-rigidity</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#main:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:scalar">3</a>.* For each conductivity define $$g_j=\gamma_j^{\,2/(n-2)}e.$$ These are smooth positive metrics on the compact manifold $\overline\Omega$. In Euclidean coordinates, <a id="scalar:energy-conversion"></a>$$\label{scalar:energy-conversion}
 \sqrt{\det g_j}\,g_j^{ab}=\gamma_j\delta^{ab},\qquad
 \int_\Omega\langle du,dv\rangle_{g_j}\,dV_{g_j}
       =\int_\Omega\gamma_j\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dx.$$ Thus the $g_j$-harmonic functions are exactly the solutions of the conductivity equation. Their boundary energy output also has the correct density: writing $\psi_j=\log\gamma_j/(n-2)$ gives $$\nu_{g_j}=e^{-\psi_j}\nu_e,\qquad
 dS_{g_j}=e^{(n-1)\psi_j}dS_e,\qquad
 \partial_{\nu_{g_j}}u\,dS_{g_j}
       =\gamma_j\partial_{\nu_e}u\,dS_e.$$ The equality of scalar local maps consequently gives equality of the density-valued energy pairings for every smooth pair supported in $\Gamma$, and hence of the maps on $H^{1/2}_{co}(\Gamma)$.

Choose a nonempty proper relatively open patch $\Gamma_0\subset\Gamma$; if $\Gamma$ is already proper one may take $\Gamma_0=\Gamma$. Theorem <a href="#main:patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:patch">1</a> gives a smooth diffeomorphism $\Phi:\overline\Omega\to\overline\Omega$ with $\Phi|_{\Gamma_0}=\mathrm{Id}$ and $g_2=\Phi^*g_1$. Therefore $$\Phi^*e=e^{2b}e,\qquad
 b=\frac{\log\gamma_2-\log\gamma_1\circ\Phi}{n-2}.$$ Lemma <a href="#scalar:boundary-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:boundary-rigidity">25</a> gives $\Phi=\mathrm{Id}$. The metric equality and positivity then imply $\gamma_1=\gamma_2$ on $\overline\Omega$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

In dimension three, by contrast, two distinct uniformly positive bounded measurable scalar conductivities on a ball, both equal to one near its boundary, can have the same full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[OpenAIRoughScalar2026](#ref-OpenAIRoughScalar2026)].

      "pdf_page": 9,

        {
          "anchor": "data:cap-figure",
          "exact_quote": "A local schematic cross-section of the common exterior cap.\nThe boundary moves only over a compact subset of the measured patch.\nThe oval is a nonempty open source set $U_0$ with closure inside $E$.\nThe proof uses the smooth graph, includin",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95]\n \\fill[gray!12] (-3,-1.25) rectangle (3,0);\n \\fill[blue!12] (-2,0) .. controls (-1.5,0) and (-1.3,1.0) .. (0,1.0)\n                 .. controls (1.3,1.0) and (1.5,0) .. (2,0) -- cycle;\n \\draw[thick] (-3,0)--(3,0);\n \\draw[thick,blue!60!black] (-2,0)\n                 .. controls (-1.5,0) and (-1.3,1.0) .. (0,1.0)\n                 .. controls (1.3,1.0) and (1.5,0) .. (2,0);\n \\draw[dashed] (-2,-0.13)--(-2,0.23);\n \\draw[dashed] (2,-0.13)--(2,0.23);\n \\node at (0,-0.7) {$M$};\n \\node at (-1,0.38) {$E$};\n \\draw[fill=white] (0.55,0.44) ellipse (0.34 and 0.19);\n \\node at (0.55,0.44) {$U_0$};\n \\node[above] at (0,1.02) {$s=-b(z)$};\n \\node[below] at (0,0) {$P_0\\subset\\Gamma$};\n \\node[right] at (3,0) {$s=0$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D cross-section diagram showing an exterior cap $E$ attached along a horizontal boundary line segment at $s = 0$. The cap region $E$ is bounded above by a curve with flat edges labeled $s = -b(z)$. Inside the shaded region $E$, there is a tilted oval labeled $U_0$. Below the horizontal line $s = 0$, the segment is labeled $P_0 \\subset \\Gamma$, with the underlying region labeled $M$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Compatible product solutions are given on the shaded cross. The drawing is schematic: each factor is a multidimensional domain, and containment of $S_{i}$ in $O_{i}$ is not required.",

      "pdf_page": 12,

          "anchor": "cross:figure",
          "exact_quote": "Compatible product solutions are given on the shaded cross.\n The $L^2$ interpolation estimates for single-variable solutions,\n with $\\gamma_1+\\gamma_2>1$, make the\n singular-function series converge on $O_1\\times O_2$.\n The drawing is schem",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,>=Stealth,\n  line cap=round,line join=round]\n  \\colorlet{crossink}{blue!45!black}\n  \\fill[crossink!12] (0,0.70) rectangle (4.2,1.30);\n  \\fill[crossink!12] (1.05,0) rectangle (1.65,3.35);\n  \\fill[crossink!25] (1.05,0.70) rectangle (1.65,1.30);\n  \\draw[black!45] (0,0) rectangle (4.2,3.35);\n  \\draw[crossink,thin] (0,0.70)--(4.2,0.70)\n    (0,1.30)--(4.2,1.30) (1.05,0)--(1.05,3.35)\n    (1.65,0)--(1.65,3.35);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,thick] (0.58,0.37) rectangle (3.66,2.85);\n  \\node[fill=white,inner sep=3pt] at (2.65,2.30) {$O_1\\times O_2$};\n  \\node[text=crossink] at (2.95,1.00) {$D_1\\times S_2$};\n  \\node[text=crossink,rotate=90] at (1.35,2.10) {$S_1\\times D_2$};\n  \\node[anchor=north] at (2.10,-0.12) {$D_1$ (first variable)};\n  \\node[rotate=90,anchor=south] at (-0.15,1.68) {$D_2$ (second variable)};\n  \\draw[->,thick] (4.60,1.68)--(5.32,1.68);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=6.10cm] at (5.60,1.68) {\n    Compatible solutions on the cross\\\\[6pt]\n    $\\displaystyle\n       \\|w\\|_{O_i}\\leq C\\|w\\|_{S_i}^{\\gamma_i}\n                              \\|w\\|_{D_i}^{1-\\gamma_i}$\\\\[6pt]\n    $\\displaystyle 0<\\gamma_i<1,\\qquad \\gamma_1+\\gamma_2>1$\\\\[8pt]\n    The cross series constructs a solution\\\\\n    on the smaller product $O_1\\times O_2$.\n  };\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic coordinate diagram of domains and product solutions. On the left is a rectangular bounding box with the horizontal axis labeled \"$D_1$ (first variable)\" and the vertical axis labeled \"$D_2$ (second variable)\". Inside the box is a shaded cross formed by a vertical strip labeled \"$S_1 \\times D_2$\" and a horizontal strip labeled \"$D_1 \\times S_2$\". An unshaded inner rectangle is labeled \"$O_1 \\times O_2$\". A horizontal arrow points from the diagram to text on the right: \"Compatible solutions on the cross\", followed by the estimate $\\|w\\|_{O_i} \\le C \\|w\\|_{S_i}^{\\gamma_i} \\|w\\|_{D_i}^{1-\\gamma_i}$ with conditions $0 < \\gamma_i < 1, \\quad \\gamma_1 + \\gamma_2 > 1$, and an arrow pointing to \"The cross series constructs a solution on the smaller product $O_1 \\times O_2$.\""
    },

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,>=Stealth,\n  line cap=round,line join=round]\n  \\colorlet{sphereink}{blue!45!black}\n  \\node at (1.70,2.40) {One fixed spatial dilation $\\lambda$};\n  \\draw[black!45,dashed] (1.70,0.55) circle (1.35);\n  \\draw[sphereink,thick] (1.70,0.55) circle (0.88);\n  \\draw[black!45,dashed] (1.70,0.55) circle (0.42);\n  \\fill (1.70,0.55) circle (1.6pt);\n  \\node[anchor=north east,inner sep=3pt] at (1.70,0.55) {$y$};\n  \\draw[->,sphereink] (1.70,0.55)--(2.3223,1.1723);\n  \\node[rotate=45,anchor=south,inner sep=3pt] at (2.011,0.861) {$r_\\delta(y)$};\n  \\fill[sphereink] (2.58,0.55) circle (1.6pt);\n  \\draw[->,sphereink,thick] (2.66,0.55)--(4.02,0.55);\n  \\node[anchor=north,align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (3.42,0.38)\n    {trace of\\\\$u_1$};\n  \\node[anchor=north,align=center] at (1.70,-1.02)\n    {$t(y)<0$,\\quad $\\delta\\downarrow0$\\\\[3pt]\n     $r_\\delta(y)\\downarrow0$};\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=7.10cm] at (4.30,0.55) {\n    For a harmonic pair $(u_1,u_2)$:\\\\[7pt]\n    $\\displaystyle\n      T_yu_1=\\int_{S_y}\\psi_\\delta(y,\\omega)\\,\n        u_1\\!\\left(\\exp_y^\\gamma(r_\\delta(y)\\omega)\\right)\n        \\,d\\sigma_y$\\\\[5pt]\n    $\\displaystyle \\hphantom{T_yu_1}{}=u_2(y).$\\\\[9pt]\n    Exact affine moments:\\\\[4pt]\n    $\\displaystyle T_y1=1,\\qquad T_yx^i=y^i.$\n  };\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A normal-coordinate section illustration. On the left are concentric circles representing geodesic spheres with center point $y$ and radius labeled $r_\\delta(y)$, surrounded by an outer dashed boundary labeled \"trace of $u_1$\". Annotations include \"One fixed spatial dilation $\\lambda$\" above, and \"$t(y) < 0, \\; \\delta \\downarrow 0$\" and \"$r_\\delta(y) \\downarrow 0$\" below. On the right, mathematical formulas are displayed: \"For a harmonic pair $(u_1, u_2)$:\", $T_y u_1 = \\int_{S_y} \\psi_\\delta(y, \\omega) u_1(\\exp_y^\\gamma(r_\\delta(y)\\omega)) \\, d\\sigma_y = u_2(y)$, and \"Exact affine moments:\", $T_y 1 = 1, \\quad T_y x^i = y^i$."
    }

{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "Boundary determination for smooth Riemannian metrics alone serves as the geometric baseline and prerequisite for the coupled problem involving an additional nonabelian gauge field.",
      "relation": "prerequisite",
      "source_anchor": "main:patch",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-365.2",
      "source_quote": "If two smooth Riemannian metrics $g_1,g_2$ on $M$ satisfy $$\\Lambda_{g_1,\\Gamma}=\\Lambda_{g_2,\\Gamma},$$",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-365.1",
      "target_quote": "For $j=1,2$, let $g_j$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$ and let $A_j\\in C^\\infty(M;T^*M\\otimes\\mathfrak u(2))$.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "This result specialises the boundary patch inverse problem to the case of a trivial connection, establishing diffeomorphism gauge uniqueness for the Riemannian metric alone.",
      "relation": "specialises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-main-patch",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-365.2",
      "source_quote": "there is a smooth diffeomorphism $\\Phi:M\\to M$",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-365.1",
      "target_quote": "then there are a smooth diffeomorphism $\\Phi:M\\longrightarrow M$",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
  ],
  "generation_status": "COMPLETE",
  "graph_revision": "store-1082",
  "pm_id": "PM-365.2",
